Hashes establish file identity. They do not establish mathematics. The
supporting results used here are the following exact mathematical gates.

**Gate R (n700, Theorem A).** Over an algebraically closed complete
nontrivially valued field, a finite continuous ordinary-zero-Hessian
function on any good separated strict boundaryless pure analytic curve
is GR-harmonic, classically pluriharmonic, G-locally real PL, and
GJR-harmonic. This includes singular and nonreduced curves, and holds on
every ordinary open. It retains canonical integration with actual
generic lengths. It grants no arbitrary-field current ascent.

**Gate T (n701, Theorems A and B).** If $K$ is complete and nontrivially
valued, $M/K$ is an algebraic valued extension, and $C=\widehat M$, then
every good separated strict boundaryless pure $n$-dimensional space,
$n\geq1$, has actual scalar extension $X_C$ with those same properties
and pure dimension $n$. For finite continuous $h$,
\[
                   D[h]=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
                   D[h\circ\pi_{C/K}]=0.                 \tag{3}
\]
The equations use all ordinary compact $(n-1,n-1)$ tests and each field's
canonical integration. Every compact ordinary form in any bidegree on
$X_C$ is the exact smooth pullback of one compact ordinary form on $X_E$
for a finite embedded $K\subseteq E\subseteq M$. These assertions hold
on every ordinary open and retain all actual structures and lengths.
No perfectness, separability, tameness, reducedness, or preimposed
upstairs equation is required. The theorem does not assert (3) for an
arbitrary analytic extension outside this completed-algebraic scope.

In n700 the smooth recognition step uses actual ordinary interiors of
strict affinoids embedded into smooth proper algebraic curves over the
same algebraically closed field. Wanner comparison is applied only there.
The supplied closed-curve, reduction, puncture-removal, and normalization
proofs then handle singular and nonreduced structures. We use the
intrinsic Theorem A, not its separate ground-field ambient-probe theorem.
In particular, no assertion about normalization smoothness over the
original imperfect $K$ is imported.

In n701, actual finite tensors, finite Galois induction, Frobenius for
mixed inseparable stages, and exact rational domains supply the finite
and completed gates. Its componentwise purity proof is separate from
mere invariance of total dimension. The proof's ordinary compact gluing
and continuous-multiplier bounds are needed; a scalar-invariant test
claim or a direct-image formula alone would not supply (3).

## The valued completed algebraic closure

**Standard finite-field foundation.** A finite algebraic extension $E/K$
of a complete non-Archimedean field admits a unique extending absolute
value, is complete in that absolute value, and every embedding of such
finite extensions over $K$ is isometric. This includes inseparable
extensions. This is BGR, *Non-Archimedean Analysis*, 3.2.4/2, used at
exactly this scope in n700, S2, and proved in n701, Section 3.1. The
uniqueness assertion follows by finite-dimensional norm equivalence and
applying a comparison bound to arbitrarily high powers. We use this
standard foundation on finite fields over the complete fields $K$ and,
in the next lemma, $L$; it is not a current theorem.

**Lemma 1.** An algebraic closure $\overline K$ has a compatible absolute
value extending that of $K$, and $C=\widehat{\overline K}$ is an
algebraically closed complete nontrivially valued analytic extension of
$K$, in every characteristic.

**Proof.** Give each finite intermediate $E/K$ its unique norm. Two
choices agree on intersections because their restrictions extend the
norm on a finite complete intermediate field. On their finite compositum
they agree with its unique norm. These norms therefore define a
multiplicative ultrametric absolute value on the union $\overline K$.
The embedding of $K$ is isometric. Its metric completion is a field:
for a nonzero limit $a$ of elements $a_m$ of $\overline K$, eventually
$|a_m|=|a|>0$, and
\[
 |a_m^{-1}-a_j^{-1}|=|a_m-a_j|/(|a_m||a_j|)
\]
shows that the inverses converge. Multiplication and the norm extend
continuously, retaining multiplicativity and the ultrametric inequality.
Completeness follows by construction. An element of $K^\times$ with
absolute value different from one retains that value in $C$, so the
valuation is nontrivial. This verifies the analytic-extension definition
in GR 2.1--2.2: a complete non-Archimedean field with an isometric
embedding of the ground field.

Here is a proof of algebraic closure, rather than an invocation of a
completion theorem. Take a monic polynomial
\[
 P(T)=T^d+\sum_{i<d}a_iT^i\in C[T],\qquad d\geq1,
 \quad R=\max(1,\max_{i<d}|a_i|).
\]
For $m\geq1$, choose monic $P_m\in\overline K[T]$ whose coefficients
have errors at most $\delta_m=2^{-md}R^{-(d-1)}$ from those of $P$.
Density allows these simultaneous choices. Every root of $P_m$ has norm
at most $R$: if $|z|>R$, the leading term has norm strictly greater
than every other term. All $P_m$ split, with repetitions allowed, in
$\overline K$.

Choose a root $b_1$ of $P_1$. Given a root $b_m$ of $P_m$,
\[
 |P_{m+1}(b_m)|\leq\delta_m R^{d-1}=2^{-md}.
\]
Factor $P_{m+1}(T)=\prod_{j=1}^d(T-c_j)$. At least one factor has
$|b_m-c_j|\leq2^{-m}$; otherwise their product would have norm
greater than $2^{-md}$. Choose it as $b_{m+1}$. The ultrametric
inequality gives $|b_j-b_m|\leq2^{-m}$ for $j>m$, so $b_m$ converges
in $C$ to $b$. Coefficient convergence and the bound $|b_m|\leq R$
give $P(b)=\lim_m P_m(b_m)=0$. Thus every nonconstant polynomial has
a root, after dividing by its leading coefficient; repeated division
gives algebraic closure. No derivative, separability, discrete valuation,
or spherical completeness was used. $\square$

**Lemma 2 (actual common field for this integration).** For every analytic
extension $L/K$ there is an actual complete nontrivially valued field $F$
with isometric embeddings of $C$ and $L$ whose restrictions to $K$ agree.

**Proof.** Choose an algebraic closure $\overline L$ and endow it with
the compatible extension norm using the complete field $L$ as in
Lemma 1. A $K$-embedding $\overline K\to\overline L$ exists: partially
defined embeddings on intermediate fields, ordered by extension, have
unions on chains. A maximal one missing an algebraic element extends by
choosing a root of that element's minimal polynomial in $\overline L$,
a contradiction. Each finite $K$-subfield embeds isometrically by the
finite-field foundation. Thus this embedding is isometric on all of
$\overline K$. Take $F=\widehat{\overline L}$. The embedding extends
isometrically from $C$ into this complete field, and $L$ already embeds
isometrically. They agree on $K$. Nontriviality is inherited from $K$.
$\square$

Lemma 2 is a direct construction for the fields appearing here. It
does not assert that $C\widehat\otimes_K L$ is a field or define any
pullback of a continuous current. It can be used in the function-level
scalar descent below. In that use we make no claim that $F/C$ is
completed-algebraic, and no current equation is transported through it.

## Published function-level inputs and their applications

All GR references below refer to **arXiv:2506.13548v2, 17 September 2025**,
W. Gubler and J. Rabinoff. The inspected primary text has SHA-256
`3a221b86116604c950db07e4c60ab184701f0624deebabd92502e5c81871436b`;
the PDF has SHA-256
`83ca9a19394f44bb2e4e1aa316de95938f311acd53a00fe250fa0f605184b726`.
The operator-selected bodies are extracted by LF alone, preserving
formfeeds: GR 3.5(7) at LF 827--885; 3.14--3.15 and 3.16 at
1062--1103; 4.1--4.2 at 1105--1177. The first unit starts with
“Proof of Proposition 3.5(7)” and ends with its printed proof square;
the last includes the complete printed proof of 4.2, not just its
statement. Their exact body hashes are, respectively,
`c2133ed53c731ad28c6d3049f47e3f3af27023f374e57fe0c06dac3570866455`,
`6faf0cbe62ec3b5cfb3d2817b457a27ad9922b80938b455d944eace90e79f793`,
and `3aaee5628a226898b74714bb8b2e15108bf61d7d0e8d3b96afeea80f7377446f`.
The primary appendix in the authoritative argument embeds these full
units and the additional definitions used below. No source ending is
replaced by a tool-output truncation.

**GR subharmonicity and harmonicity.** Definition 3.4 starts with an
upper semicontinuous function on a separated pure analytic curve $Z$.
For every nontrivially valued analytic extension $E/k$ and every map
from a smooth connected strict $E$-curve into $Z_E$, its composite is
Thuillier-subharmonic or identically $-\infty$. Definition 3.13 says a
real-valued function is harmonic if both signs are subharmonic.
GR 3.4.2 and 3.12 identify these finite two-sign functions on smooth
strict curves over nontrivially valued fields with Thuillier harmonic
functions. A finite function never uses the identically-$-\infty$
alternative.

**GR Proposition 3.14(6).** Precisely: if $Z$ is a $k$-analytic curve,
$u:Z\to\mathbb R$ is continuous, and $E/k$ is an analytic extension
field, then $u$ is harmonic if and only if
$u\circ\pi_{E/k}:Z_E\to\mathbb R$ is harmonic. There is no assumption
of reducedness, smoothness, strictness, perfectness, characteristic,
or algebraicity of $E/k$. This is the two-sign specialization of
Proposition 3.5(7), for upper semicontinuous functions. We apply it
first with $Z=X$ and $E=C$, **after** Gate R recognizes $h_C$.

We have checked the complete scalar-extension proof, rather than
reading “if and only if” as current functoriality. Its reverse direction
uses a simultaneous analytic extension and, on a smooth strict probe,
the comparison definition of Thuillier subharmonicity. In our application
the simultaneous extension of $C$ and any other $L/K$ is explicitly
constructed in Lemma 2. The proof then compares already subharmonic
functions, never continuous currents.

The exact smooth foundations in that proof are: Thuillier Definition
3.1.5 compares an upper semicontinuous function, not identically
$-\infty$ on any component, with harmonic functions on every strict
affinoid domain; Thuillier Proposition 2.3.18 preserves the harmonic
sheaf on a smooth strict curve under any non-Archimedean extension;
and GR Lemma 2.12 states for **any** analytic space $U$ and analytic
extension $E/k$ that
\[
                         \pi_{E/k}(\partial U_E)=\partial U.       \tag{4}
\]
Scalar projection is surjective (GR 2.9, CLD 11.1.9). The affinoid
harmonic pullback used in GR's comparison step has the same continuous
boundary values, via the strict-affinoid harmonic convention; the printed
proof applies Proposition 2.3.18 at precisely this step. Immediately before
that proposition, Thuillier defines
$H(U)=\Gamma(U\setminus\partial U,H_Y)\cap C^0(U,\mathbb R)$ for an
affinoid domain of a smooth curve $Y$, and extends this convention to its
G-site. The supporting Lemma 2.3.5 states exactly: for a quasi-compact
simply semistable formal curve $\mathfrak U$ over $k^\circ$ and any
non-Archimedean extension $E/k$, a continuous function on
$\mathfrak U_\eta$ belongs to $H(\mathfrak U)$ if and only if its scalar
pullback belongs to
$H(\mathfrak U\times_{\operatorname{Spf}(k^\circ)}\operatorname{Spf}(E^\circ))$.
Its full primary proof is retained as an additional unit. This is a
function and skeleton theorem; we do not give an arbitrary affinoid a
semistable presentation by assertion. The published GR scalar theorem
is the result imported for general curves. These assertions concern
harmonic functions and relative boundaries, not an equality of all
analytic interiors.

For clarity, the comparison step is as follows. On the smooth strict
probe $Y$ over $L$, take a strict affinoid $U\subseteq Y$ and a
harmonic $a$ on $U$ with $v\leq a$ on $\partial U$. After passing to
the common field, the recognized subharmonic function $v_F$ and harmonic
$a_F$ satisfy the same boundary inequality by (4). Thuillier comparison
gives $v_F\leq a_F$ on $U_F$. Surjectivity then gives $v\leq a$ on
$U$. Each sign gives the required two-sign property. Smoothness and
strictness are those of the probe in Definition 3.4; their preservation
under complete scalar extension is the standard base-change property
of quasi-smoothness (CLD 11.4.8--9), together with boundarylessness.
If componentwise purity needs to be checked for the particular
$Y_F$ used here, $F=\widehat{\overline L}$ is exactly an instance of
Gate T over $L$, applied **only to geometry**, not to any current.
Finite values exclude the $-\infty$ component alternative in the
printed proof. Thus the source's use of component surjectivity to
exclude that alternative is immaterial for these applications.

This reconstructs the applicable function-level logic of the published
scalar detection result. We import that result in its exact published
scope; we do not extrapolate the arbitrary-current gate to $F/C$.

**GR curve pullback and classical psh.** Proposition 3.14(7) states
that for any morphism of analytic curves $f:Y\to Z$, harmonic $u$
pulls back to harmonic $u\circ f$; its converse requires $f$ finite
surjective. We use only forward curve pullback in the new integration.
Proposition 3.5(8) gives the corresponding forward subharmonic pullback.
Definition 4.1 requires, for every analytic extension $L/k$, every
$L$-analytic curve $Y$, and every $f:Y\to Z_L$, subharmonicity of
$u\circ\pi_{L/k}\circ f$. GR 4.1.2 proves that on an analytic curve
classical psh is equivalent to subharmonicity, using precisely
3.5(7,8). It covers all analytic test curves, not just the smooth
strict probes used to define subharmonicity. We apply it separately
to $h$ and $-h$.

GR Proposition 4.2(5), also fully supplied, is a second valid route:
classical psh functions on **any** analytic space are preserved and
detected by every analytic field extension. Once Gate R has made both
signs of $h_C$ classically psh, it too descends the same conclusion.
It is a consistency check here, not an additional current premise.
We do not use the finite-map converse in 4.2(6), although its full
source proof is retained in the selected unit.

**GR Proposition 3.15 and GJR.** Precisely: on a strict analytic curve
over a nontrivially valued field, a continuous function is GR-harmonic
if and only if it is real PL and its residual line bundle
$L_h(x)\in\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{red}(X,x))_{\mathbb R}$
is numerically trivial for every $x$. The statement has no
reducedness or perfectness requirement. Its full printed proof uses
completed perfection, reduction, and normalization to reduce to the
rig-smooth case, and descends already recognized harmonicity and PL
data. We apply this **published proposition itself**, not a claim that
its intermediate currents ascend. Our $K$ is nontrivially valued,
$X$ is strict and good, and $h$ is continuous and now harmonic, so all
its hypotheses hold. GR 2.16 fixes the G-local analytic-unit convention.

The GJR primary version is *Forms on Berkovich spaces based on harmonic
tropicalizations*, arXiv:2111.05741v3, 7 March 2025, the inspected
final-published-version snapshot. Its field convention 2.2 requires
complete nontrivial valuation. Section 5 and Definition 5.1 concern good
strict analytic spaces and G-covers by strict affinoid domains, with
finite sums $\sum\lambda_j\log|f_j|$ of analytic units. Definition 7.2
defines harmonicity of a real-PL function by both-sign semipositivity
at every point. Remark 7.3(1) equates that condition with numerical
triviality of $L_h(x)$. Our ground-field space satisfies these exact
field, goodness, and strictness hypotheses. Proposition 3.15 first
supplies the real-PL condition, and only then do we apply the GJR
criterion. No ordinary smooth moment representation follows from this.

## Actual geometry, compact tests, and calibration at the current gate

Choose $C$ as in Lemma 1, with $M=\overline K$. This is an actual
algebraic valued extension and its metric completion, so Gate T applies
with $n=1$. On a strict affinoid $V=\mathcal M(A)$ the source upstairs
is $\mathcal M(A\widehat\otimes_K C)$, with the actual completed
algebra, including nilpotents.

Goodness follows from inverse images of ordinary interiors of affinoid
neighbourhoods; strict presentations remain strict after scalar
extension. Separatedness follows by base change of the closed diagonal.
Boundarylessness follows from the relative-interior base-change
inclusion, CLD 11.4.1 or Berkovich 1993, Proposition 1.5.5(iii),(iv),
as used in Gate T. We use only that inclusion, not equality of arbitrary
absolute interiors. Purity of **every** actual upstairs component and
local dimension one follow from n701's separate componentwise purity
proof in Sections 3.2, 6 and 9. Merely knowing
$\dim X_C=\dim X=1$ would be insufficient.

The function $h_C=h\circ\pi_{C/K}$ is finite and continuous. A compact
set downstairs has compact inverse image: take a finite cover by
ordinary interiors of affinoid neighbourhoods, whose scalar inverse
images are compact; the inverse image of the compact set is closed in
their finite union since the carriers are Hausdorff. This is the
compact-support assertion in n701 Section 3.3, with no finite-fibre
assumption on $C/K$.

For an arbitrary $\beta\in A_c^{0,0}(X_C)$, n701 Theorem A gives a
finite embedded $E\subseteq\overline K$ and
$\alpha_E\in A_c^{0,0}(X_E)$ with $\beta=q_E^*\alpha_E$ **as
smooth sections**, on the entire space. This includes zero regions,
ordinary presentation opens and compact zero extensions. Its smooth
Hessian is therefore exactly $q_E^*D\alpha_E$. Finite-stage transport
in n701 gives $D[h_E]=0$. Canonical smooth-top-measure projection,
CLD Proposition 15.3.1, gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \int_{X_C}h_C(D\beta)_{\mathrm{Rad},C}
 &=\int_{X_C}(h_E\circ q_E)(q_E^*D\alpha_E)_{\mathrm{Rad},C}\\
 &=\int_{X_E}h_E(D\alpha_E)_{\mathrm{Rad},E}=0.
 \end{aligned}                                         \tag{5}
\]
The multiplier is bounded on the compact support. This is an
application of a theorem proved for all compact tests. It is not a
claim that an arbitrary continuous current has been pulled back.

The length bookkeeping is essential. In the calibration underlying
(5), a finite Artinian moment-fibre algebra $B$ over a complete field
$H$ has dimension $d$. After a complete extension $H'/H$, its tensor
is already complete as a finite-dimensional vector space and decomposes
into local factors $B_j$. A composition series in each factor gives
\[
 d=\dim_{H'}(B\otimes_H H')
   =\sum_j\operatorname{length}_{B_j}(B_j)[\kappa_j:H'].     \tag{6}
\]
Each composition factor is the residue field of its local Artinian
ring, so this calculation permits nilpotents, inseparable residue
extensions, and unequal lengths. It is the actual degree sum in CLD
13.3.3 and 14.3.6, used in n701 Section 10, and supplies the canonical
projection formula with **no field-degree factor**. Individual generic
weights may change under scalar extension; their appropriate calibrated
sums are retained. They are never normalized to one or divided out.

In contrast, CLD Remark 14.4.3 concerns a finite $E$-analytic space
viewed as $K$-analytic on the **same carrier**: its $K$-calibration is
$[E:K]$ times the $E$-calibration. n701 uses that different statement
only after constructing an actual local $E$-analytic structure in
its finite-Galois induction. We do not replace (5) by that statement,
and never assign an infinite degree factor to $C/K$.

## Proof of the theorem and all quantifiers

If $X$ is empty the conclusions are vacuous. Otherwise take the valued
completed algebraic closure $C$ of Lemma 1. The original $K$ and $X$
satisfy every hypothesis of Gate T: complete nontrivial valuation,
goodness, separatedness, strictness, boundarylessness, and pure
dimension one. The preceding application checks give a good separated
strict boundaryless **pure analytic curve** $X_C$, with its actual
structure, and the equation $D[h_C]=0$ against every ordinary compact
scalar test.

The field $C$ is algebraically closed, complete, and nontrivially valued.
Gate R now applies directly to $X_C$ and its finite continuous multiplier.
It gives GR-harmonicity of $h_C$. Its singular and nonreduced cases are
part of that exact theorem, so no smoothness or reduction assumption on
$X_C$ is needed and none is silently introduced. In particular, possible
new nilpotents from geometric inseparability cause no scope failure.

Apply GR Proposition 3.14(6) with $Z=X$, $k=K$, $k'=C$, and $u=h$.
$X$ is a separated pure analytic curve, $h$ is continuous, and $C/K$
is an analytic extension. The proposition detects harmonicity of this
**already recognized** upstairs function and gives GR-harmonicity of $h$.
This is precisely the published reverse function-level implication;
it does not detect or ascend an arbitrary current.

Consequently $h$ and $-h$ are subharmonic in Definition 3.4. GR 4.1.2
applies on the analytic curve $X$ to each sign, making both signs
classically psh. Definition 7.1 gives classical pluriharmonicity. To
spell out its full scope, fix any analytic extension $L/K$, any
$L$-analytic curve $Y$, and any $f:Y\to X_L$. Definition 4.1 for each
sign gives subharmonicity on $Y$ of the corresponding sign of (2).
The composite is finite and continuous; Definition 3.13 makes it
GR-harmonic. This includes the further extension-field and smooth-probe
quantifiers already inside subharmonicity on $Y$. If $Y$ is smooth and
strict, GR 3.4.2 and 3.12 identify it with Thuillier harmonicity. No
current equation on $X_L$, on $Y$, or on any common extension has been
used in this quantifier step. The field $L$ may be transcendental over
$K$, and the map $f$ may be inseparable, nonfinite, or constant.

Now apply GR Proposition 3.15 on the original strict $K$-curve. Its
nontrivial-valuation hypothesis and continuity hypothesis hold, and
GR-harmonicity was just established. It gives real PL in the published
G-local convention and numerical triviality of every residual class.
GJR Definition 7.2 and Remark 7.3(1), with the complete nontrivially
valued $K$ and good strict $X$, give GJR-harmonicity.

Finally let $\Omega\subseteq X$ be an ordinary open. It is an analytic
domain (CLD 11.1.7). It is good, separated and strict by ordinary-local
restriction and the strict-neighbourhood statement CLD 11.7.7; local
dimension is preserved by domain restriction (11.2.3), so it is pure
one-dimensional if nonempty. Its open inclusion has full relative
interior, and composition with the boundaryless structural morphism
makes it boundaryless (11.4.1). A compact scalar test on $\Omega$
extends by zero to a compact ordinary scalar test on $X$: its support
is a compact closed subset of the Hausdorff $X$ contained in the open
$\Omega$, and every exterior point has a neighbourhood disjoint from
that support. Canonical integration and differentials restrict to
domains (CLD 14.3.5, 15.7.2 and Section 7.7), so (1) restricts.
Apply the same theorem to $\Omega$, or use harmonicity's sheaf property
GR 3.14(1). These are actual ordinary opens at every analytic point,
not merely one affinoid or an admissible cover. This proves all claims.
$\square$

## Comparison with the original residual scope

The catalogue's published curve recognition case was an ordinary open
in a smooth proper algebraic curve's analytification over an
algebraically closed complete nontrivially valued field. Gate R already
extends that to all good separated strict boundaryless pure analytic
curves over that field. The new integration removes the algebraic-closure
restriction **for every such actual analytic curve**, including
imperfect-field and singular or nonreduced cases. It needs no product,
invariance, factorization, imposed scalar-stability, finite covering
presentation, or algebraic compactification downstairs.

This is a new stated special scope of original P06.2, not its full
arbitrary-dimensional completion. No original-scope analytic
counterexample is constructed. The all-dimensional Gate T supplies
current ascent only through a completion of an algebraic extension;
Gate R supplies recognition only in dimension one. Their composition
does not recognize arbitrary higher-dimensional nonproduct germs.
Ordinary opens of a curve are covered; arbitrary nonproduct
higher-dimensional domains remain outside this conclusion. The
non-strict invisible-boundary example does not meet this theorem's
strict hypotheses.

The two original proof obligations remain distinct. The current-ascent
obligation used here is exactly the completed-algebraic gate. The
recognition obligation is then closed by intrinsic curve recognition
and published harmonic-function descent. An ordinary CLD-smooth
decreasing psh approximation of either sign is a stronger construction,
and no such construction is supplied or needed by this route. We make
no general positive-current regularization claim and no ordinary smooth
harmonic-approximation claim. No new arbitrary-transcendental current
ascent, reverse-current characterization, or all-extension zero-current
theorem is claimed. Harmonicity of every field-extended curve composite
is a function-level conclusion.

No round23 incomplete/nullproof, old inconclusive arbitrary-field
recognition, or old full-curve two-sign approximation assertion is a
dependency. The independent round25 smoothing campaign is neither read
as support nor modified. The quoted 4.2(6) finite-map converse is not
used to bypass any missing image or component hypothesis.

```text
Proof of Proposition 3.5(7). It is immediate from the definition that subharmonicity of
u implies subharmonicity of u ◦ πk′ /k . Suppose then that u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic. Let
k′′ /k be another analytic extension field with k′′ non-trivially valued, and let f : Y →
X k′′ be a morphism from a smooth, connected, strictly k′′ -analytic curve. Assume that
u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ̸≡ −∞, so we need to show that u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic in Thuillier’s
sense. Let K be a non-Archimedean field that is simultaneously an analytic extension of
k′ and k′′ 2.2, and consider the following commutative diagram:

                               ←   fK            πK/k′
                                                 ←
                          YK            → XK          → X k′
                          ←



                                           ←



                                                            ←




(3.10.1)                                     πK/k′′             πk′ /k
                          →

                               ←                 ←                ←
                           Y       f
                                       → X k′′         →
                                                  πk′′ /k   X            u → R ∪ {−∞}.
             CLASSICALLY PSH AND PLURIHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON BERKOVICH SPACES                 17

Since u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic, the composition u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K is, on each connected compo-
nent C of YK , either subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense or identically −∞; since C surjects
onto Y [Duc18, 1.5.6] and u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ̸≡ −∞, the latter does not happen. Replacing
X by Y and k′ /k by K/k′′ , we are reduced to showing that if X is smooth and strictly
k-analytic for k non-trivially valued and u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense, then
so is u.
   We can do this directly using Thuillier’s definition. (The converse is the difficult di-
rection: see [Thu05, Corollaire 3.4.5].) Let U ⊂ X be a strictly k-affinoid domain and
let h: U → R be a harmonic function such that u ≤ h on ∂ U. Then h ◦ πk′ /k : Uk′ =
π−1
  k′ /k
        (U) → R is harmonic by [Thu05, Proposition 2.3.18]. We have πk′ /k (∂ Uk′ ) = ∂ U by
Lemma 2.12, so u ◦ πk′ /k ≤ h ◦ πk′ /k on ∂ Uk′ . Since u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic in Thuillier’s
sense, this implies u ◦ πk′ /k ≤ h ◦ πk′ /k on Uk′ , so u ≤ h on U since πk′ /k : Uk′ ↠ U is
surjective. Thus u is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense, as claimed.                          □
```

### GR3.14--3.15

GR.txt, LF 1062--1103; SHA-256 `6faf0cbe62ec3b5cfb3d2817b457a27ad9922b80938b455d944eace90e79f793`.

```text
Proposition 3.14. Let X be a k-analytic curve and let h: X → R be a continuous function.
     (1) (Sheafiness) The harmonic functions form a sheaf on X .
     (2) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX× ) then log | f | is harmonic.
     (3) The harmonic functions on X form a real vector space.
     (4) (Limits) If h is locally a uniform limit of harmonic functions, then it is harmonic.
     (5) (Maximum Principle) If h is harmonic and h attains a local extremum at x ∈ Int(X ),
         then h is constant in a neighborhood of x.
     (6) (Extension of Scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. Then h is harmonic
         if and only if h ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R is harmonic.
     (7) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic curve and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If h is
         harmonic then h ◦ f is harmonic, and the converse holds if f is finite and surjective.
     (8) (Boundaries) If h is harmonic on Int(X ), then it is harmonic on X .
   We will later see in Theorem 7.6 that (4) holds even for pointwise limits. The fol-
lowing proposition asserts that Definition 3.13 is equivalent to the notion of harmonicity
in [GJR21, Definition 7.2]. We assume that k is non-trivially valued as in ibid.
Proposition 3.15. Let X be a strictly k-analytic curve and let h: X → R be a continuous
function. The function h is harmonic if and only if it is R-PL and the line bundle Lh (x) ∈
Pic(red(X , x))R defined in (2.17) is numerically trivial for every point x ∈ X .
Proof. This is [GJR21, Proposition 15.7] in the rig-smooth case; we reduce to this case
                                                                         −∞
in the usual way. If char(k) = p > 0 let k′ be the completion of k p , and otherwise let
k′ = k. Let X ′ = X ⊗b k k′ and let π: X ′ → X be the structure morphism. Let ι : X red
                                                                                      ′
                                                                                        ,→ X ′
be the underlying reduced space and let ν: Xe ′ → X red  ′
                                                            be the normalization, so that Xe ′ is
rig-smooth. Let f : Xe ′ → X denote the composition π ◦ ι ◦ ν.
   Suppose that h is harmonic. Then h ◦ f is harmonic by Proposition 3.14(6,7), so h ◦ f
is R-PL and Lh (x ′ ) is numerically trivial for all x ′ ∈ Xe ′ by the rig-smooth case. Then
h is R-PL by Lemma 3.9(6) and [GJR21, Lemma 5.3(3), Corollary 5.11], and Lh (x) is
numerically trivial for all x ∈ X by [GJR21, Proposition 7.6(1′ , 2′ )]. Conversely, suppose
that h is R-PL and that Lh (x) is numerically trivial for all x ∈ X . The same holds for
h ◦ f by [GJR21, Proposition 7.6(1′ , 2′ )], so h ◦ f is harmonic by the rig-smooth case, and
hence h is harmonic by Proposition 3.14(6,7).                                                  □
             CLASSICALLY PSH AND PLURIHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON BERKOVICH SPACES               21

Remark 3.16. We still assume that X is a strictly k-analytic curve over a non-trivially
valued field k and that h: X → R is continuous. Proposition 3.15 and Proposition 3.14(6)
imply in particular that if h◦πk′ /k is harmonic for some analytic extension field k′ /k, then
h◦πk′′ /k is R-PL for any analytic extension k′′ /k, which is by no means obvious. Likewise,
Proposition 3.14(7) implies that if f : Y → X is finite and surjective and h◦ f is harmonic,
then h is R-PL, and Proposition 3.14(8) implies that if h|Int(X ) is harmonic then h is R-PL.
```

### GR4.1--4.2

GR.txt, LF 1105--1177; SHA-256 `3aaee5628a226898b74714bb8b2e15108bf61d7d0e8d3b96afeea80f7377446f`.

```text
                     4. CLASSICALLY PLURISUBHARMONIC FUNCTIONS
  In this section we define the notion of a classically plurisubharmonic function. The
definition follows Definition 3.4.
Definition 4.1 (Classically Plurisubharmonic Functions). Let X be a k-analytic space and
let u: X → R ∪ {−∞} be an upper semicontinuous function. We say that u is classically
plurisubharmonic or classically psh provided that, for every analytic extension field k′ /k,
every k′ -analytic curve Y , and every morphism f : Y → X k′ , the function u◦πk′ /k ◦ f : Y →
R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic on Y in the sense of Definition 3.4.
4.1.1. Any class of psh functions should respect extension of scalars and should be stable
under pullback, so the conditions in Definition 4.1 are necessary. In complex analysis,
this condition (without the extension of scalars) is the definition of plurisubharmonicity,
which is why we call the notion “classically psh”.
4.1.2. If X is a k-analytic curve, then a function u: X → R∪{−∞} is classically psh if and
only if it is subharmonic. Indeed, if u is classically psh then taking k′ = k and Y = X in
Definition 4.1 shows that u is subharmonic, and if u is subharmonic then it is classically
psh by Proposition 3.5(7,8).
4.1.3. In Definition 4.1, one can assume that Y is a connected, smooth, strictly k′ -analytic
curve: this follows from Definition 3.4.
  Classically psh functions satisfy the basic properties of subharmonic functions on curves.
Proposition 4.2. Let X be a k-analytic space.
   (1) (Sheafiness) The classically psh functions form a sheaf on X .
   (2) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX ) then log | f |: X → R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh.
   (3) (Cone Property) If u1 , u2 : X → R∪{−∞} are classically psh and λ1 , λ2 ∈ R≥0 then
       λ1 u1 + λ2 u2 and max{u1 , u2 } are classically psh.
   (4) (Limits) The infimum of a decreasing net of classically psh functions is classically
       psh. For arbitrary pointwise limits, we refer to Theorem 6.5.
   (5) (Extension of Scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. A function u: X →
       R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh if and only if u ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R ∪ {−∞} is classically
       psh.
   (6) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic space and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If
       u: X → R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh then u ◦ f is classically psh, and the converse
       holds if f is finite and surjective.
Proof. Properties (1,3,4) follow immediately from Proposition 3.5(1,4,5). For Proposi-
tion 4.2(2), we consider an analytic function f on X . As | f | is continuous, we deduce
22                                  W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

that log | f | is a continuous function with values in R ∪ {−∞} and hence it is upper semi-
continuous. Then Proposition 4.2(2) follows from Proposition 3.5(2).
    To prove Proposition 4.2(5), it is clear from the definitions that if u is classically psh,
then u ◦ πk′ /k is classically psh for every analytic extension field k′ /k. To prove the con-
verse, we assume that k′ /k is an analytic extension field and that u′ := u ◦ πk′ /k is a
classically psh function on X k′ . Since the morphism πk′ /k is closed and surjective 2.9,
it is clear that u is upper semicontinuous. Let k′′ /k be an analytic extension field and
let f : Y → X k′′ be a morphism over k′′ from a k′′ -analytic curve Y . We have to show
that u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic. As in the proof of Proposition 3.5(7), we consider
a simultaneous analytic extension field K of k′ and k′′ . It follows from the above that
u ◦ πK/k = u′ ◦ πK/k′ is classically psh and hence u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K is subharmonic. We con-
clude from the commutative diagram (3.10.1) that u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K = u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ◦ πK/k′′ is
subharmonic and hence u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic by Proposition 3.5(7).
    Likewise, the proof of Proposition 4.2(6) follows the same lines as the proof of Propo-
sition 3.5(8). Again, it follows from the definitions if u is a classically psh function on X ,
then u ◦ f is a classically psh function on Y . Conversely, we assume that u ◦ f is a clas-
sically psh function for a finite surjective morphism f : Y → X . Since finite morphisms
are proper and hence closed, we deduce from upper semicontinuity of u ◦ f and from
surjectivity of f that u is upper semicontinuous. It remains to show that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g is
subharmonic for any analytic extension field k′ /k and any morphism g : Z → X k′ from a
connected k′ -analytic curve Z. Setting W := Z ×X k′ Yk′ , we get a morphism g ′ : W → Yk′
from f by base change with respect to f ′ : Yk′ → X k′ as in (3.10.2). Since u ◦ f is classi-
cally psh, it follows from Proposition 4.2(5) that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ′ = u ◦ f ◦ πk′ /k is classically
psh. The morphism f is finite and surjective, hence the same holds for f ′ . We con-
clude that the base change f ′′ : W → Z of the morphism f ′ is finite and surjective. The
k′ -analytic space W has irreducible components of dimension 1 and maybe isolated irre-
ducible components of dimension 0. Removing the latter, we obtain a k′ -analytic curve
W ′ as an open and closed subset of W . Since Z is a connected k′ -analytic curve and f ′′
is a finite surjective map from W onto Z, we get an induced finite surjective morphism
W ′ → Z. Indeed, as W ′ is also Zariski-closed in W , we deduce from finiteness of f ′′ that
f ′′ (W ′ ) is a Zariski-closed subset in Z of dimension 1 and hence f ′′ (W ′ ) = Z as Z is a
connected k′ -analytic curve. Since the inclusion W ′ → W is obviously finite, the com-
position W ′ → Z is also finite. In the following, we replace W by W ′ , which preserves
the commutativity of the diagram (3.10.2) and has the advantage that W is a k′ -analytic
curve. It follows that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ′ ◦ g ′ = u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g ◦ f ′′ is subharmonic on W . The
morphism of k′ -analytic curves f ′′ : W → Z is finite and surjective, so we deduce from
Proposition 3.5(8) that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g is subharmonic.                                           □
```

### GR field conventions

GR.txt, LF 175--186; SHA-256 `457bc55b838876a08f6d41dd3948ec1f525fc28d939a64e92d4d6145228940c2`.

```text
2.1. General notation and conventions. In this paper, k will always denote a non-
Archimedean field, that is, a field k equipped with a non-Archimedean complete absolute
value | |: k → R, which may be trivial.
  The set of natural numbers N includes 0. We allow equality in a set-theoretic inclusion
S ⊂ T . For an abelian group P, let PΛ := P ⊗Z Λ. For a ring A, let A× be the group of
invertible elements.
  A topological space is called compact if it is quasi-compact and Hausdorff.
 2.2. Ground field and extensions. The valuation ring of k is denoted k◦ := {α ∈ k |
 |α| ≤ 1}, its unique maximal ideal is k◦◦ := {α ∈ k | |α| < 1}, and its residue field is
ek := k◦ /k◦◦ . An analytic extension field is a non-Archimedean field k′ endowed with an
 isometric embedding k ,→ k′ . For analytic field extensions k′ and k′′ of k, there is a joint
 analytic field extension F of k′ and of k′′ : see 2.8.
```

### GR actual joint analytic fields and scalar spaces

GR.txt, LF 254--273; SHA-256 `3db8bc8468fea029166b4e2598d763c0badaeeb707cfd5aedf634493193256ee`.

```text
2.8. Fiber products. The category of k-analytic spaces admits fiber products by [Ber93,
§1.4]. For analytic spaces X , Y over a k-analytic space S, we denote the fiber product by
X ×S Y . Given x ∈ X and y ∈ Y over the same point s ∈ S, there always exists z ∈ X ×S Y
mapping to x and to y with respect to the canonical projections [Duc18, 1.2.13]. For
convenience, we repeat the argument. The completed tensor product H (x)⊗         b H (s) H ( y)
contains H (x) ⊗H (s) H ( y) by a result of Gruson [Gru66, §3.2 Théorème 1(4)], so
H (x)⊗b H (s) H ( y) ̸= 0. The Berkovich spectrum of any nonzero Banach ring is nonempty
                                                                 
[Ber90, Theorem 1.2.1], and any z ′ ∈ M H (x)⊗     b H (s) H ( y) induces a point z ∈ X ×S Y
mapping to x and y. Note that H (z) is a simultaneous analytic extension field of H (x)
and of H ( y).
  Since Gruson’s result holds for Banach algebras over a non-Archimedean field, the
same argument shows that for two analytic field extensions of k, there is a joint analytic
extension of both.

2.9. Extension of scalars. Let k′ /k be an an analytic extension field. Then there is a
base extension functor (·)k′ which associates to a k-analytic space X a k′ -analytic space
X k′ , given on a k-affinoid space X = M (A ) by the k′ -affinoid space X k′ = M (A ⊗b k k′ )
[Ber90, §1.4]. We denote by πk′ /k : X k′ → X the structure map, which is closed and
surjective by [GRW17, Lemma 2.19], [Duc24, (3.1.1.2)].
```

### GR relative boundaries and full Lemma 2.12

GR.txt, LF 302--390; SHA-256 `2e820525db8b3713d077bb2e01f654e600438fc455cc464efc1f9e2ffd4c5466`.

```text
2.11. Relative boundary. An important notion in this paper is that of the relative bound-
ary of a morphism ϕ : X → Y of k-analytic spaces. This is a closed subset of X which we
denote by ∂ (X /Y ). We will not give the details of this definition, but let us try to explain
the literature on the subject. In [Ber90, §2.5], Berkovich defines the relative boundary
∂ (X /Y ) for k-affinoid spaces X and Y . Then in [Ber90, §3.1], he generalizes ∂ (X /Y ) for
good k-analytic spaces using that all points of X and Y have k-affinoid neighborhoods.
Berkovich calls ϕ closed if ∂ (X /Y ) = ;. Berkovich defines ∂ (X /Y ) for arbitrary k-analytic
spaces in [Ber93, 1.5.3(ii)], as follows. A morphism ϕ is closed if for every morphism
Y ′ → Y from a good k-analytic space Y ′ , the fiber product X ′ := X ×Y Y ′ is good and the
canonical morphism X ′ → Y ′ is closed in the previous sense. Finally in [Ber93, Defini-
tion 1.5.4], a point x ∈ X is defined to be in Int(X /Y ) if x has an open neighborhood U
such that ϕ induces a closed morphism U → Y in the above sense. We refer to [Tem15,
Definition 4.2.4.1] for a more explicit equivalent definition of ∂ (X /Y ). Note that in this
paper, we will use the notion closed map in the usual topological sense meaning that it
maps closed sets to closed sets. This is different from Berkovich’s terminology.
   The complement Int(X /Y ) := X \ ∂ (X /Y ) is called the relative interior. We will usually
apply this in the case Y = M (k), in which case we call ∂ X := ∂ (X /M (k)) the boundary
of X and Int(X ) := X \ ∂ X the interior. If ∂ X = ;, we say that X boundaryless. A bound-
aryless space is good by definition. Note that Berkovich calls boundaryless k-analytic
spaces closed.
   We list here some properties of the boundary and interior which we will use throughout
the paper:
   (1) A point x ∈ X is contained in Int(X /Y ) if and only if there is an open neighbor-
       hood U of x such that Int(U/Y ) = U: this is part of the above definition.
   (2) If ϕ : X → Y is finite, then Int(X /Y ) = X [Ber93, Example 1.5.3(iii)].
   (3) If X is an analytic domain in Y , then Int(X /Y ) is the topological interior of X in
       Y [Ber93, Proposition 1.5.5(i)].
             ϕ     ψ
   (4) If X → Y → S are morphisms of k-analytic spaces, then

(2.11.1)                   Int(X /Y ) ∩ ϕ −1 Int(Y /S) ⊂ Int(X /S)

        [Ber93, Proposition 1.5.5(ii)], with equality if ψ is locally separated [Tem04,
        Corollary 5.7]. Taking S = M (k), this becomes

(2.11.2)                      Int(X /Y ) ∩ ϕ −1 Int(Y ) ⊂ Int(X ).

We will need the following lemma, which is a consequence of deep results in [CT21] in
general, although it has an elementary proof for good spaces.
Lemma 2.12. If X is a k-analytic space and k′ /k is an analytic field extension, then
                                          πk′ /k (∂ X k′ ) = ∂ X .
Proof. We have πk′ /k (∂ X k′ ) ⊂ ∂ X by 2.11(5′ ). Let x ∈ ∂ X , and let U ⊂ X be a compact
neighborhood of x. We claim that x ∈ ∂ U. If not, then there is an open neighborhood
V of x in U such that ∂ V = ; by 2.11(1′ ). If V ′ ⊂ V is a smaller open neighborhood of
x in X , then ∂ V ′ = ; as well by 2.11(3′ ) and by (2.11.4) as applied to V ′ → V → M (k).
Thus we may assume that V is open in X , which implies by 2.11(1) that x ∈ Int(X ), a
contradiction. Hence x ∈ ∂ U. If x ′ ∈ π−1      k′ /k
                                                      (x), then x ′ ∈ ∂ Uk′ if and only if x ′ ∈ ∂ X k′
for the same reason. We need to show π−1        k′ /k
                                                      (x) ∩ ∂ X k′ ̸= ;, so we may replace X by U to
assume that X is compact. In this case, the closed subset ∂ X k′ is compact; suppose that
it is disjoint from π−1
                      k′ /k
                            (x). Then πk′ /k (∂ X k′ ) is a closed subset of X not containing x, so
there exists an open neighborhood V of x such that Vk′ = π−1            k′ /k
                                                                              (V ) ⊂ Int(X k′ ). This means
that ∂ Vk′ = ; by 2.11(3,4). But x ∈ ∂ V , and boundarylessness can be checked after
extension of scalars by [CT21, Theorem 11.5]. This contradiction proves the claim. □
```

### GR real PL convention

GR.txt, LF 416--431; SHA-256 `c30a5a23188cfc7e14645e2e73e4ec8c8a374163732de3fe815591d462b04435`.

```text
2.16. PL functions. We will assume k is non-trivially valued when we discuss PL func-
tions. Let Λ be a subgroup of R which is either divisible or equal to Z. We consider a
good, strictly k-analytic space PX . A function h: X → R is called Λ-piecewise linear or
Λ-PL if G-locally we have h = j λ j log | f j | for finitely many λ j ∈ Λ and invertible ana-
lytic functions f j . Here we use the G-topology of X generated by the strictly k-analytic
domains. We refer to [GJR21, Section 5] for more on Λ-PL functions.

2.17. Reduction of germs. For a good, strictly k-analytic space X over a non-trivially
valued field, Temkin [Tem00] introduced the reduction of the germ (X , x) at x ∈ X as
an open subset of a Zariski–Riemann space PHf(x)/ek of H    f(x)/ek. Roughly speaking, it
is the projective limit of all canonical reductions Spec(A  f) where M (A ) ranges over
all k-affinoid neighborhoods of x. The germ (X , x) is naturally a quasicompact locally
ringed space. See also [CD12, Section 6.1] for an excellent summary. An R-PL function
h: X → R induces a residue line bundle L(h) ∈ Pic(X , x)R . This was introduced in [CD12],
and is explained in [GJR21, Section 6]. We call h semipositive at x if L(h) is nef in the
sense of [GJR21, Section 7]. A semipositive R-PL function is semipositive at all x ∈ X .
```

### GR curves, subharmonic definition and Proposition 3.5

GR.txt, LF 453--522; SHA-256 `9dcb2805b2ec6b27e3bf02e0a53f1c0b2c7315348e4784b83535ec01c56e0cd8`.

```text
  In this section, we generalize Thuillier’s theory [Thu05] of subharmonic functions from
smooth strictly analytic curves to arbitrary k-analytic curves.
3.1 (k-Analytic Curves). By a k-analytic curve we mean a separated k-analytic space of
pure dimension 1. We gather the following facts about k-analytic curves:
   (1) A k-analytic curve is good, i.e., every point has an affinoid neighborhood. [Duc24,
        Proposition 3.3.7]
   (2) A k-analytic curve is paracompact. [Duc24, Théorème 4.5.10]
   (3) If X is an affinoid k-analytic curve, then ∂ X coincides with the Shilov boundary
        of X . [Thu05, 2.1.2]
An analytic domain in a k-analytic curve (for example, an open subset) is again a k-
analytic curve.
3.2 (Thuillier’s Harmonic Functions). Thuillier [Thu05, §2.3] defines a sheaf of harmonic
functions on a smooth, strictly k-analytic curve for a non-trivially valued k, roughly as
follows. If X is affinoid, then a continuous function h: X → R is harmonic if, after passing
to a finite, separable field extension, it coincides with the composition of a harmonic
function on a skeleton of X (in the sense of graph theory) with the retraction to the
skeleton. If X is smooth, then a harmonic function on an open subset of X is defined to be
a continuous function whose restriction to every strictly affinoid subdomain is harmonic.
3.3 (Thuillier’s Subharmonic Functions). For a non-trivally valued k, Thuillier [Thu05,
Définition 3.1.5] defines a subharmonic function on a smooth, strictly k-analytic curve X
to be an upper semicontinuous function u: X → R ∪ {−∞} that is not identically −∞
on any connected component of X and that satisfies the following property: for every
strictly k-affinoid domain U ⊂ X and every harmonic function h: U → R, if u ≤ h on ∂ U
then u ≤ h on U.
  Now we extend this definition to the case of non-smooth curves. The idea is that a
function should be subharmonic if it pulls back to a subharmonic function along any
morphism from a smooth curve. However, an inseparable curve over an imperfect field
does not admit any non-constant morphisms from a smooth curve, so one has to allow
an extension of scalars as well. We also consider the constant function −∞ to be sub-
harmonic, following Demailly [Dem85] in the complex case.
Definition 3.4 (Subharmonic functions). Let X be a k-analytic curve and let u: X →
R ∪ {−∞} be an upper semicontinuous function. We say that u is subharmonic provided
that, for every non-trivially valued analytic extension field k′ /k, every smooth, connected,
strictly k′ -analytic curve Y , and every morphism Y → X k′ , the composition
                                        πk′ /k     u
                              Y −→ X k′ −→ X −→ R ∪ {−∞}
             CLASSICALLY PSH AND PLURIHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON BERKOVICH SPACES              11

is either subharmonic on Y in the sense of [Thu05, Définition 3.1.5], or is identically −∞.
3.4.1. If X is a smooth, connected, strictly k-analytic curve over a non-trivially valued k,
then an upper semicontinuous function u: X → R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic in our sense
if and only if it is either subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense or is identically −∞. Indeed, if
u is subharmonic in our sense then one can take k = k′ and Y = X to see that it is either
subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense or is identically −∞. Conversely, if u is identically −∞
then it is clearly subharmonic in our sense, and if u is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense
then it is subharmonic in our sense by [Thu05, Proposition 3.1.14 and Corollaire 3.4.5].
3.4.2. As a special case of (3.4.1), if X is a smooth, connected, strictly k-analytic curve
over a non-trivially valued k, then a function u: X → R (taking finite values) is subhar-
monic in our sense if and only if it is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense.
  Here we collect the basic properties of subharmonic functions, which we will prove
over the course of this section.
Proposition 3.5. Let X be a k-analytic curve and let u: X → R ∪ {−∞} be an upper
semicontinuous function.
   (1) (Sheafiness) The subharmonic functions form a sheaf on X .
   (2) (Analytic functions) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX ) then log | f | : X → R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic.
   (3) (Boundaries) If u is subharmonic on Int(X ), then u is subharmonic on X .
   (4) (Cone Property) If u1 , u2 : X → R ∪ {−∞} are subharmonic and λ1 , λ2 ∈ R≥0 then
       λ1 u1 + λ2 u2 and max{u1 , u2 } are subharmonic.
   (5) (Limits) The infimum of a decreasing net of subharmonic functions is subharmonic.
   (6) (Maximum Principle) If u is subharmonic and u attains a local maximum at x ∈
       Int(X ), then u is constant in a neighborhood of x.
   (7) (Extension of scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. Then u is subharmonic
       if and only if u ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R ∪ {∞} is subharmonic.
   (8) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic curve and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If u
       is subharmonic then u ◦ f is subharmonic, and the converse holds if f is finite and
       surjective.
   (9) (Finite Values) If u is subharmonic and u(x) = −∞ for a non-rig-point x ∈ X ,
       then u ≡ −∞ on the irreducible component of Int(X ) ∪ {x} containing x.
  Let us make some remarks regarding the content of Proposition 3.5.
3.5.1. If U ⊂ X is an open subset then U is again a k-analytic curve, so it makes sense
```

### GR two signs and harmonic definition

GR.txt, LF 1042--1061; SHA-256 `318b4943b3933359360281150a468635684abb39a253ab4213bea738c8d91cca`.

```text
3.12. Now we turn to harmonic functions. Let X be a smooth, strictly k-analytic curve
with k non-trivially valued and let h: X → R be a function. We claim that h is harmonic
in the sense of (3.2) if and only if h and −h are subharmonic (in our sense or Thuillier’s—
see (3.4.2)). This is implicit in Thuillier’s thesis, but it requires justification. Suppose then
that h: X → R is harmonic. Then ±h are subharmonic by [Thu05, Proposition 2.3.13 and
Corollaire 3.1.12]. Conversely, suppose that ±h are subharmonic. This implies that h is
continuous. Let U ⊂ X be a strictly affinoid domain. By [Thu05, Lemme 2.3.9], there
exists a unique harmonic function h′ : U → R that coincides with h on ∂ U. By definition
20                                 W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

of subharmonicity, we have h ≤ h′ on U. By the same argument as applied to −h, we see
that h = h′ on U, so that h is harmonic on U. As U was arbitrary, h is harmonic.
  By 3.12, the following definition recovers Thuillier’s definition of harmonicity on a
smooth, strictly k-analytic curve, and even on a strictly affinoid rig-smooth curve us-
ing [Thu05, Corollaire 2.3.14].
Definition 3.13 (Harmonic functions). Let X be a k-analytic curve for a possibly trivially
valued non-Archimedean field k. A function h: X → R is harmonic if h and −h are both
subharmonic.
   The advantage of this definition is that the following basic properties of harmonic func-
tions follow immediately from the corresponding properties of subharmonic functions.
```

### GJR PL definition and domain conventions

GJR.txt, LF 1628--1661; SHA-256 `105c91a086cb4d4bb27760f4bcbed6bd8bfa19bbd1b817109ac725b11a27921e`.

```text
to fix our ideas. We will find it useful to work slightly more generally with piecewise
linear functions with slopes contained in an additive subgroup Λ ⊂ R, and in some cases
new arguments are needed. In practice we will usually take Λ = Z, Q, or R, in which case
either Λ = Z or Λ is divisible.
   In this section, K is a non-Archimedean field with a non-trivial valuation v and value
group Γ := v(K × ) ⊂ R. We consider a good strictly K-analytic space X and an additive
subgroup Λ ⊂ R. Recall from 2.3 that a strictly analytic space is good provided that that
every point has a strictly affinoid neighbourhood. Most strictly analytic spaces occurring
in practice are good: for example, any strictly affinoid space, a space with no boundary,
or an analytic subdomain of the analytification of an algebraic variety. We restrict our
attention to good spaces as then the coherent modules on the Berkovich space X are
the same as the coherent modules on the space X G endowed with the G-topology [Ber93,
Proposition 1.3.4], which makes it reasonable to work with line bundles on X . We always
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                       31

use the G-topology on X generated by the strictly affinoid domains. Let C(X ) be the space
of continuous real functions on X .
Definition 5.1. A function h: X → R is called piecewise Λ-linear or Λ-PL if there exists a
                                                                                                    ×
G-covering {XP i } of X such that for each i, there are finitely many λi j ∈ Λ and f i j ∈ O (X i )
with h|X i = j λi j log | f i j |. A Z-PL function is called piecewise linear or PL.
  We denote the group of PL functions on X by PL(X ), and the group of Λ-PL functions
by PLΛ (X ). These are subgroups of C(X ).
5.2. Some remarks:
   (1) A function h: X → R is PL if and only if there exists a G-covering {X i } of X and
       f i ∈ O (X i )× such that h|X i = log | f i |.
   (2) The set of Λ-PL functions on X forms a group since any two G-coverings {X i } and
       {X ′j } admit the common refinement {X i ∩ X ′j }.
   (3) The restriction of a Λ-PL function to a strictly analytic domain is Λ-PL.
   (4) The presheaf U 7→ PLΛ (U) is a sheaf in the G-topology, and therefore in the ana-
       lytic topology as well.
   (5) A function h: X → R is Λ-PL if and only if there exists a G-covering {X i } of
       X suchPthat for each i, there are finitely many λi j ∈ Λ and hi j ∈ PL(X i ) with
       h|X i = j λi j hi j .
```

### GJR harmonic definition and numerical triviality

GJR.txt, LF 2331--2346; SHA-256 `224d6b851b30d23490090985374ba845adea25b889f7b6c2b940d10f5c163da7`.

```text
Definition 7.2. A Λ-PL function h: X → R is called semipositive at a point x ∈ X if it
satisfies the equivalent conditions of Proposition 7.1, and it is harmonic at x if both h
and −h are semipositive at x. We say that h is semipositive if it is semipositive at all
points of X .
   A harmonic function on X is an R-PL function h: X → R which is harmonic at all points
of X . A Λ-PL harmonic function is called Λ-harmonic.
7.3. Some remarks:
   (1) A Λ-PL function h is harmonic at x ∈ X if and only if Lh (x) is numerically trivial.
       Equivalently, there exists a paracompact neighbourhood U of x, a formal K ◦ -
       model U of U, and a numerically trivial element L ∈ M (U)Λ , such that h = hL .
   (2) Semipositivity and harmonicity are insensitive to the group Λ, in the following
       sense: if h ∈ PLΛ (X ), then h is semipositive at a point x if and only if its image in
       PLR (X ) is semipositive at x.
   (3) Semipositivity and harmonicity of h at x is intrinsic to the germ of h in PLΛ (X , x).
       In particular, the semipositive (resp. harmonic) functions on X form a sheaf in
       the analytic topology. (They do not form a sheaf in the G-topology).
```

## Full imported dependency n700

Origin: Published round07, SHA 8c46bccc1c2dd4286d3ccfc42aea70698ef8e449b9e57f962d707ecffd953a04, full-evidence independent PASS38/66 in round06/P06.2-curve-recognition-embedded-evidence-review; complete supporting closed-curve proof retained in this body.

title: Curve recognition and actual local analytic images
author: Author self-audited partial research draft
date: 3 October 2026

## Status, conventions, and exact statements

This is a new, unverified partial submission for P06.2. Its proof bytes have
an author self-audit only. No old review verdict is transferred to them, and
no formal verification or priority claim is made. The original atom remains
open. The blocked preceding workspace has been preserved without edits.

All spaces and morphisms are Berkovich analytic spaces over the field
specified in each statement. An ordinary open is open in the underlying
topology. An analytic neighbourhood contains an ordinary neighbourhood;
an analytic domain alone need not do so. A curve is separated and pure of
analytic dimension one. Put $D=d'd''$. All forms and currents in an equation
below are **ordinary** Chambert--Loir--Ducros (CLD) forms and currents,
defined on ordinary opens. For a pure space $Z$, $[u]=u\delta_Z$ uses
canonical integration with the actual generic artinian lengths of its
structure, including nilpotents. In dimension one,
\[
                 D[u](\psi)=\int_Z uD\psi,
                 \qquad \psi\in A_c^{0,0}(Z).
\]
The equation is tested against every compact ordinary smooth scalar test.
Smooth top forms define signed Radon measures, so these integrals with a
continuous multiplier are well defined. This is not an a priori assertion
that the differentiated current $D[u]$ is a measure.

Here ``harmonic'' on an arbitrary curve means Gubler--Rabinoff (GR)
Definition 3.13: both signs are subharmonic in GR Definition 3.4, which
includes analytic field extensions. On a smooth strict curve over a
nontrivially valued field this agrees with Thuillier harmonicity. Classical
pluriharmonicity means that both signs are classically psh in GR Definition
4.1, with its **every analytic extension and every analytic curve map**
quantifiers. Gubler--Jell--Rabinoff (GJR) harmonicity is the real-PL
residual numerical-triviality notion; it is not ordinary CLD smoothness.
Real PL is used in the precise GR 2.16/GJR convention: G-locally on
strict analytic domains the function is a finite real linear combination
of logarithms of analytic units. The local real-PL conclusions below
refer to that convention, not to one ordinary smooth moment expression.

**Theorem A (intrinsic curve recognition).** Let $K$ be algebraically
closed, complete, and nontrivially valued. Let $Z$ be a good separated
strict boundaryless pure analytic curve. If $u:Z\to\mathbb R$ is continuous
and $D[u]=0$, then $u$ is GR-harmonic, classically pluriharmonic, locally
real PL, and GJR harmonic. If $Z$ is smooth, $u$ is Thuillier harmonic.
There is no reducedness, normality, properness, or global algebraic-model
hypothesis. The theorem also applies on every ordinary open of $Z$.

**Proposition B (local images, arbitrary field).** Let $K$ be complete and
nontrivially valued, $X$ good separated strict boundaryless, and
$f:Y\to X$ a $K$-analytic morphism from a good separated smooth strict
boundaryless pure curve. At each analytic point $y\in Y$, either:

1. on an ordinary neighbourhood of $y$, $f$ factors analytically through
   $\mathcal M(E)$ for a finite field extension $E/K$, mapping to a single
   rigid point of $X$; or
2. there are strict affinoid neighbourhoods $V$ of $y$ and $U$ of $f(y)$,
   with both points in their ordinary interiors, such that $V$ is connected
   and normal and $V\to U$ is finite. Its reduced kernel image
   $C\hookrightarrow U$ is irreducible and pure one-dimensional,
   $V\to C$ is finite surjective, and it admits a finite analytic lift to
   the finite normalization of $C$.

**Theorem C (ground-field ambient probes).** Let $K$ be algebraically
closed, complete, and nontrivially valued. Let $X$ be good separated strict
boundaryless and pure of dimension $n\geq1$. If $h:X\to\mathbb R$ is
continuous with $D[h]=0$, then, for every $K$-analytic map $f:Y\to X$ as
in Proposition B, $h\circ f$ is Thuillier harmonic on $Y$.

Theorem C is fieldwise. It does **not** say that $h$ is classically
pluriharmonic on $X$, or that the equation ascends to $X_L$. Even when
$K$ is algebraically closed, analytic extensions $L/K$ can be
transcendental. The universal conclusion of Theorem A is justified instead
by the already-recognized curve harmonicity and the precise GR curve
functoriality theorem.
