### primary-16: CLD 11.4.1: relative-interior composition equality

Cached primary CLD-v2.txt, lines 15506 to 15534. The next primary heading is the extraction boundary; no output-budget truncation was used. Source typography and OCR artifacts are retained.

```text
11.4.1. Bord et intérieur. — Soit f : Y → X un morphisme d’espaces k-analytiques.
On note Int(Y /X) l’intérieur relatif de f et ∂(Y /X) son complémentaire, le bord relatif
de f ([9], Definition 1.5.4). Par construction, Int(Y /X) est un ouvert de Y . Si ∂(Y /X) =
0, nous dirons que f est sans bord (la terminologie de [9] est closed ). Lorsque f est
l’inclusion d’un domaine analytique, Int(Y /X) coïncide avec l’intérieur Y̊ de Y dans X
au sens de la topologie générale et ∂(Y /X) coïncide donc avec le bord topologique de Y
dans X ([9], Prop. 1.5.4).
   Si X est un schéma localement de type fini sur une algèbre affinoïde A, le morphisme
structural de X an vers M (A) est sans bord.
   Si g : Z → Y et f : Y → X sont deux morphismes d’espaces k-analytiques alors ([9],
Prop. 1.5.5)
(11.4.1.1)                  Int(Z/Y ) ∩ g −1 (Y /X) ⊆ Int(Z/X),
et l’on a
(11.4.1.2)                  Int(Z/Y ) ∩ g −1 (Y /X) = Int(Z/X),
si f est localement séparé ([127], Corollary 5.7), ce qui est notamment le cas dès que Y
est séparé.
   Lorsque X = M (k), on note Int(Y ) = Int(Y /k) et ∂(Y ) = ∂(Y /k), qu’on appelle
l’intérieur et le bord de Y respectivement. Nous dirons que Y est sans bord si ∂(Y ) = ∅.
Si Y est sans bord, ou plus généralement si f : Y → X est sans bord et si X est bon,
l’espace Y est bon. Si f : Y → X est un morphisme d’espaces k-analytiques et que X
est séparé, le cas 11.4.1.2 entraîne les égalités
(11.4.1.3)    Int(Y /X) ∩ f −1 (Int(X)) = Int(Y ) et ∂(Y ) = ∂(Y /X) ∪ f −1 (∂(X)).
   Si Y → X est un morphisme d’espaces k-analytiques et si si X ′ est un espace analy-
tique sur une extension complète de k muni d’un k-morphisme vers X, on a l’inclusion
Int(Y /X) ×X X ′ ⊂ Int(Y ×X X ′ /X ′ ) (cela découle de la proposition 3.13 de [8]). La
classes des morphismes sans bord est en particulier stable par changement de base ana-
lytique sur k. Elle est stable par composition d’après 11.4.1.2 et est G-locale au but
([127], Cor. 5.6).
```

### primary-17: CLD 11.4.4: actual finite-module Banach algebras

Cached primary CLD-v2.txt, lines 15567 to 15602. The next primary heading is the extraction boundary; no output-budget truncation was used. Source typography and OCR artifacts are retained.

```text
11.4.4. Morphismes finis. — Soit A une algèbre k-affinoïde. Une A-algèbre de Ba-
nach finie est une A-algèbre de Banach B qui est finie comme A-algèbre, et dont la
norme est équivalente à la norme quotient induite par une présentation finie An → B
du A-module B. Dans ce cas la norme de B est en fait équivalente à la norme quotient
associée à n’importe quelle présentation finie du A-module B (c’est une conséquence de
[8], Prop. 2.1.9). Toute A-algèbre de Banach finie est k-affinoïde (combiner [20], Prop.
6.1.3/4 et [8], Cor. 2.1.8). Le foncteur d’oubli de la norme induit une équivalence de
catégories entre A-algèbres de Banach finies et A-algèbres finies(2) ([8], Prop. 2.1.12).
   Soit f : Y → X un morphisme entre espaces k-analytiques. Si X et Y sont affinoïdes,
on dit que f est fini si f ∗ fait de O(Y ) une O(X)-algèbre de Banach finie ; cela revient
à demander que f soit sans bord ([8], Cor. 2.5.13). En général (si l’on ne suppose plus
X et Y affinoïdes) les assertions suivantes sont équivalentes : ([9], Cor. 1.3.7).
  (i) Il existe un G-recouvrement (Xi ) de X par des domaines affinoïdes tels que pour
tout i, l’image réciproque f −1 (Xi ) soit affinoïde et que le morphisme induit de f −1 (Xi )
vers Xi soit fini
  (ii) Pour tout domaine affinoïde V de X l’image réciproque f −1 (V ) est affinoïde et le
morphisme induit de f −1 (V ) vers V est fini.

  Si |k × | ̸= {1} et si A est strictement k-affinoïde, on déduit de [8], Prop. 2.1.1 et [20], Thm. 6.1.3/1
(2)

que toute A-algèbre de Banach qui est finie comme A-algèbre est une A-algèbre de Banach finie. Par
contre si k est trivialement valué et si 0 < r < s < 1, le morphisme continu naturel de k{T /s} vers
k{T /r} est bijectif (sa source et son but s’identifient tous deux à k[[T ]]) mais sa réciproque n’est pas
bornée, si bien que k{T /s} n’est pas une k{T /r}-algèbre de Banach finie).

312                CHAPITRE 11. RAPPELS DE GÉOMÉTRIE ANALYTIQUE

On dit que f est fini si elles sont satisfaites ; cette définition est compatible avec la
précédente si X et Y sont affinoïdes. Un morphisme fini est sans bord.
  La classe des morphismes finis est stable par composition et changement de base
analytique sur k, et la propriété d’être fini est G-locale au but.
  Soit y un point de Y . On dit que f est fini en y s’il existe un voisinage analytique
U de f (y) dans X et un voisinage analytique V de y dans f −1 (U ) tel que f induise un
morphisme fini de V vers U . Si c’est le cas le point y appartient à Int(Y /X), et si de
plus X et Y sont bons OY,y est un OX,x -module de type fini. La finitude en un point est
stable par composition et changement de base analytique sur k.
```

### primary-19: CLD 13.3.1-3: degree and Artinian base change, complete lemma proof

Cached primary CLD-v2.txt, lines 19143 to 19191. The next primary heading is the extraction boundary; no output-budget truncation was used. Source typography and OCR artifacts are retained.

```text
13.3.1. — Soit f : Y → X un morphisme d’espaces k-analytiques. Soit y ∈ Y . Si
dimy (f −1 (f (y)) > 0, on pose degy (f ) = +∞. Sinon, le point y est isolé dans sa fibre
f −1 (f (y)), l’anneau local de f −1 (f (y)) en y est une H (f (y))-algèbre de dimension finie
et on note degy (f ) sa dimension.
   Lorsque f est fini et plat en y, cet entier degy (f ) coïncide avec l’entier degy (f ) défini
en 11.6.3.
   Nous dirons que degy (f ) est le degré de f en y.
   Soit V un domaine analytique de Y contenant y et soit U un domaine analytique
de X tel que f (V ) ⊂ U ; soit g : V → U le morphisme déduit de f . Pour tout y ∈ V , on
a degy (f ) = degy (g), car pour tout y ∈ V d’image x ∈ X, les anneaux locaux en y de
f −1 (f (y)) et de g −1 (g(y)) coïncident.
   Soit Z un sous-espace analytique fermé de X contenant x et soit g : Y ×X Z → Z le
morphisme déduit de f par changement de base. Comme les espaces H (x)-analytiques
f −1 (x) et g −1 (x) coïncident, on a degy (f ) = degy (g).
```

```text
14.3.2. — Soit Σ une partie squelettique de X, paracompacte et contenue dans le
polyèdre caractéristique Σf d’un moment f : V → T défini sur un domaine analytique V
de X. Nous allons définir un calibrage µΣ de Σ.
   Soit C une décomposition cellulaire de Σ en f -cellules commodes (lemme 14.1.5).

q ◦f est fini et plat sur C̊ et son degré x 7→ degx (q ◦f ) prend une valeur finie, strictement
positive et constante dC sur C̊. Notons e = |e1 ∧ · · · ∧ en | le vecteur-volume canonique
de Rn , considéré comme un calibrage constant, et posons µC = dC (q ◦ f )∗trop (e) ; c’est
un calibrage standard de la cellule C. Il ne dépend pas du choix de q d’après la propo-
sition 14.3.1. La famille (µC ) définit un calibrage standard de Σ ; on le note µΣ,f,C .
   Vérifions que ce calibrage est indépendant du choix de (V, f, C ).
   Soit V ′ un domaine analytique de X, soit f ′ : V ′ → T ′ un moment tel que Σ ⊂ Σf ′ et
soit C ′ une décomposition cellulaire de Σ en f ′ -cellules commodes. Posons W = V ∩ V ′
et soit g le moment (f, f ′ ) sur W et considérons une décomposition cellulaire D de Σ en

   Soit D une cellule de dimension n appartenant à D. Soit q : T → Gnm un morphisme
de tores tel que qtrop est injectif sur ftrop (D). Notons aussi p : T × T ′ → T la première
projection.
   Soit C une cellule de C contenant D. Comme D̊ ne rencontre pas ∂(W ), il est contenu
dans Int(W/V ), si bien que q ◦ f |W est fini et plat de degré dC en tout point de D̊. Il
existe aussi un entier dD > 0 tel que (q ◦ p) ◦ g soit fini et plat de degré dD en tout point
de D̊ (proposition 14.2.2). Comme on a (q ◦ p) ◦ g = q ◦ f |W , on a dC = dD . Par ailleurs,
(q ◦ g)∗trop (e) = (q ◦ f )∗trop (e). Par suite, les triplets (V, f, C ) et (W, g, D) induisent le
même calibrage sur D.
   Cela entraîne que le calibrage µΣ,f,C de Σ déduit de (V, f, C ) coïncide avec celui déduit
de (W, g, D) ; par symétrie, il coïncide avec celui déduit de (V ′ , f ′ , C ′ ). Notons-le µΣ .

408          CHAPITRE 14. CALIBRAGE CANONIQUE D’UN ESPACE ANALYTIQUE

14.3.3. — Soit Σ′ un sous-espace paralinéaire de Σ. Soit C ′ une décomposition cellu-
laire de Σ en f -cellules commodes qui raffine C ∩ Σ′ . La description de µΣ′ ,f,C ′ entraîne
alors l’égalité µΣ |Σ′ = µΣ′ .
Lemme 14.3.4. — Soit X un espace analytique de niveau n et soit Σ une partie sque-
lettique de X. Il existe un unique calibrage µΣ de Σ vérifiant la propriété suivante : pour
tout domaine analytique V de X, tout moment f sur V et tout sous-espace paralinéaire Σ′
de Σf ∩ Σ qui est paracompact, on a µΣ |Σ′ = µΣ′ . Ce calibrage µΣ est standard.
Démonstration. — Par définition, l’espace squelettique Σ est G-recouvert par des sous-
espaces paralinéaires Σ′ comme dans l’énoncé. Les calibrages formant un G-faisceau, il
existe au plus un tel calibrage. Pour démontrer son existence, il suffit alors de vérifier
la condition de coïncidence. Or, si Σ′ est Σ′′ sont des sous-espaces paralinéaires de Σ
satisfaisant les conditions de l’énoncé, on a µΣ′ |Σ′ ∩Σ′′ = µΣ′ ∩Σ′′ = µΣ′′ |Σ′ ∩Σ′′ d’après le
paragraphe 14.3.3.

Définition 14.3.5. — Soit X un espace analytique de niveau n, topologiquement sé-
paré, et soit Σ une partie squelettique de X. On appelle calibrage canonique de Σ le
calibrage dont le lemme 14.3.4 affirme l’existence et l’unicité.
   Par construction, ce calibrage est standard. Si Σ′ ⊂ Σ, on a µΣ |Σ′ = µΣ′ . Il résulte
aussi des constructions que si Σ est contenu dans un domaine analytique V de X, le
calibrage canonique µΣ de Σ, vu comme partie squelettique de V , coïncide avec son
calibrage canonique comme partie squelettique de X.
   Ce calibrage canonique munit l’espace vertébré associé à l’espace analytique X d’une
structure d’espace G-tropical calibré de niveau n. Nous supposerons toujours implicite-
ment que X est muni de cette structure.
```

```text
Remarque 14.4.3. — Soit F une extension finie de k et soit X un espace F -analytique
de niveau n. On peut aussi voir X comme un espace k-analytique de niveau n ; pour
éviter les confusions nous le noterons ici X0 ; on a dim(X0 ) = dim(X).
```

### primary-27: CLD 15.2.2: good Hausdorff implies local holomorphic separation

Cached primary CLD-v2.txt, lines 21556 to 21571. The next primary heading is the extraction boundary; no output-budget truncation was used. Source typography and OCR artifacts are retained.

```text
15.2.2. Séparation holomorphe. — On dira qu’un espace analytique X est holo-
morphiquement séparé si pour tout couple (x, y) de points de X avec x ̸= y il existe une
fonction analytique f sur X telle que |f (x)| ̸= |f (y)|. Un tel espace est topologiquement
séparé.
  Si X est un espace holomorphiquement séparé, il en va trivialement de même de tout
domaine analytique de X.
  Tout espace affinoïde est par définition holomorphiquement séparé ; en conséquence,
tout domaine analytique d’un espace affinoïde est holomorphiquement séparé.
  Un espace analytique sera dit localement holomorphiquement séparé s’il est topologi-
quement séparé et si chacun de ses points possède un voisinage analytique holomorphi-
quement séparé.
  Un espace analytique topologiquement séparé qui est bon, ou plus généralement dont
tout point possède un voisinage isomorphe à un domaine analytique d’un espace affi-
noïde, est localement holomorphiquement séparé. C’est en particulier le cas d’un espace
analytique topologiquement séparé sans bord.
```

### primary-30: CLD 15.7.1-2: pure analytic tropical structure and domain compatibility

Cached primary CLD-v2.txt, lines 22041 to 22062. The next primary heading is the extraction boundary; no output-budget truncation was used. Source typography and OCR artifacts are retained.

```text
Théorème 15.7.1. — Soit X un espace analytique purement de dimension n, topolo-
giquement séparé. Muni de son bord ∂(X) au sens de la théorie des espaces analytiques,
l’espace G-tropical affiné et calibré de niveau n associé à X est un espace tropical de
niveau n.

Démonstration. — Vérifions les conditions de la définition 6.6.2.
  L’intérieur Int(X) de X est localement holomorphiquement séparé (15.2.2) ; il est donc
localement différentiellement séparé (corollaire 15.2.4). Il est décent (proposition 12.4.7)
car il est sans bord, donc bon, donc presque bon. Il est très riche (théorème 12.11.1) et
harmonieux (théorème 15.6.1).
  Et il résulte du corollaire 12.9.14 que Σ ∩ ∂X est n-négligeable pour toute partie
paralinéaire Σ de X, de même que Σ∩∂(V /X) pour tout domaine V de X car ∂(V /X) ⊂
∂V .
  On munit ainsi tout espace analytique X, topologiquement séparé, et purement de
dimension n, d’une structure d’espace tropical de niveau n.

              § 15.7. L’ESPACE TROPICAL ASSOCIÉ À UN ESPACE ANALYTIQUE              433

15.7.2. — Soit X un espace analytique topologiquement séparé, purement de dimen-
sion n et soit V un domaine de X. Le domaine V est muni de deux façons, a priori
distinctes, d’une structure d’espace tropical de niveau n, soit comme espace analytique,
soit comme domaine de l’espace tropical X. La formule ∂(V ) = (∂(X) ∩ V ) ∪ ∂(V /X)
montre que ces deux structures coïncident.
```

### primary-31: CLD A.2.3-4: supporting compact-cutoff argument, full proofs

```text
Lemme A.2.3. — Supposons que A (X) sépare les points. Soit K un sous-ensemble
compact de X et soit x ∈ X K. Il existe une fonction lisse sur X qui vaut 1 au voisinage
de x et nulle au voisinage de K.
Démonstration. — On commence par traiter le cas où K est réduit à un point y. Par
hypothèse, il existe une fonction lisse f sur X telle que f (x) ̸= f (y). On choisit une
fonction lisse u sur R qui est constante égale à 0 au voisinage de f (x) et constante égale
à 1 au voisinage de y ; l’existence de telles fonctions est classique. La fonction u ◦ f sur X
est lisse et convient.
   D’après ce qui précède, pour tout y ∈ K, il existe une fonction lisse fy sur X valant 1
au voisinage de x et nulle dans un voisinage ouvert Vy de    Qy. Soit S une partie finie de K
telle que les Vy , pour y ∈ S, recouvrent K ; la fonction y∈S fy convient.

Corollaire A.2.4. — On suppose que A sépare localement les points. Soit K une
partie compacte de X et soit U un voisinage ouvert de K. Il existe une fonction lisse
sur X qui vaut 1 au voisinage de K et dont le support est compact et contenu dans U .
Démonstration. — On commence par traiter le cas où le compact K est réduit à un
point x. Soit V un voisinage compact de x tel que A (V̊ ) sépare les points de V̊ . L’en-
semble F = V (V̊ ∩U ) est une partie fermée de X contenue dans V . Soit f une fonction
lisse sur V qui vaut 1 au voisinage de x et qui est nulle au voisinage de F . Soit g la
fonction sur X qui coïncide avec f sur V et qui est nulle en dehors de V . Elle est lisse
sur l’intérieur de V , sur l’ouvert X V ainsi que sur un voisinage ouvert de F dans X ;
ces trois ouverts recouvrent X. La fonction g est donc lisse. Son support est contenu
dans U ; comme il est contenu dans V , il est aussi compact.
   Traitons maintenant le cas général. Pour tout x ∈ K, soit fx une fonction lisse sur X
qui vaut 1 au voisinage de x et dont le support est compact et contenu dans U . Quitte à
remplacer fx par son carré, on suppose que cette fonction est positive ou nulle. Comme K
est compact, la somme g d’une sous-famille finie de ces fonctions est strictement positive
en tout point de K. Elle est lisse et son support est compact et contenu dans U . Soit φ
une fonction lisse de R vers [0; 1], nulle en 0 et égale à 1 sur un voisinage de g(K) ; la
fonction f = φ ◦ g convient.
```

### primary-33: Berkovich 1.5.3-5: boundaryless finite maps and base-change interiors

```text
      1.5.3. Examples. — (i) If P is the class of all morphisms of good analytic spaces,
then the morphisms from P are said to be good. For example, finite morphisms and
locally closed immersions are good morphisms.
       (ii) If P is the class of closed morphisms of good analytic spaces ([Ber], p. 49),
then the morphisms from P are said to be closed. For example, finite morphisms and
locally closed immersions are closed morphisms.
       (iii) I f P is the class of proper morphisms of good analytic spaces ([Ber], p. 50),
then the morphisms from P are said to be proper. It follows from the definitions that a
morphism is proper if and only if it is compact and closed. For example, finite morphisms
are closed. Conversely, if a proper morphism has discrete fibres, then it is finite
([Ber], 3.3.8).

      1.5.4. Definition. — The relative interior of a morphism 9 : Y -> X is the set Int(Y/X)
of all points y e Y for which there exists an open neighborhood i^ofjy such that the
induced morphism i^ -> X is closed. The complement of Int(Y/X) is called the relative
boundary of 9 and is denoted by ^(Y/X). IfX ==^(^), these sets are denoted by Int(Y)
and (?(Y) and are called the interior and the boundary of Y, respectively.
      It follows from the definition that ^(Y/X) = 0 if and only if the morphism y is
closed. The following properties of the relative interior are easily deduced from the
definition and [Ber], 3.1.3.
```

# Primary source evidence supplied directly

# Fallible new curve-recognition integration route

Original P06.2 remains open. This proposal was prepared after round25
admission and is not injected into that worker or the graph worker.

For an arbitrary complete nontrivially valued K and an actual good separated
strict boundaryless pure analytic curve X, let h be finite continuous with
ordinary D[h]=0, retaining actual generic Artinian lengths. Take C to be the
completion of a valued algebraic closure of K. The reviewed completed-
algebraic transport theorem should give D[h_C]=0 with all actual ordinary
tests and pure-curve geometry retained. The independently reviewed
algebraically-closed intrinsic curve theorem should then recognize h_C as
GR-harmonic. GR Proposition3.14(6) DETECTS already recognized harmonicity
through every analytic field extension, hence potentially descends it to h.
GR4.1.2 then gives both signs classically psh with ALL analytic extensions
and ALL curve maps; GR3.15 may give the stated G-local real-PL/GJR consequence.

This proposed recognition chain does not assume ordinary-current pullback
through a transcendental extension. It uses the published functoriality of
already harmonic functions. First-stage ordinary smooth decreasing psh
approximation is a stronger separate construction and is not claimed here.
The original arbitrary-dimensional recognition problem is still open.

Both caller-imported internal dependencies were independently reviewed in
their exact scopes; all full proof bodies are supplied, not only verdicts.
Reuse those exact gates and audit the NEW applications. Do not silently
extrapolate either dependency. Source-dependent uncertain steps remain gaps.
The original source criteria allow precise published standard foundations.

```text
Proof of Proposition 3.5(7). It is immediate from the definition that subharmonicity of
u implies subharmonicity of u ◦ πk′ /k . Suppose then that u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic. Let
k′′ /k be another analytic extension field with k′′ non-trivially valued, and let f : Y →
X k′′ be a morphism from a smooth, connected, strictly k′′ -analytic curve. Assume that
u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ̸≡ −∞, so we need to show that u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic in Thuillier’s
sense. Let K be a non-Archimedean field that is simultaneously an analytic extension of
k′ and k′′ 2.2, and consider the following commutative diagram:

Since u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic, the composition u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K is, on each connected compo-
nent C of YK , either subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense or identically −∞; since C surjects
onto Y [Duc18, 1.5.6] and u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ̸≡ −∞, the latter does not happen. Replacing
X by Y and k′ /k by K/k′′ , we are reduced to showing that if X is smooth and strictly
k-analytic for k non-trivially valued and u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense, then
so is u.
   We can do this directly using Thuillier’s definition. (The converse is the difficult di-
rection: see [Thu05, Corollaire 3.4.5].) Let U ⊂ X be a strictly k-affinoid domain and
let h: U → R be a harmonic function such that u ≤ h on ∂ U. Then h ◦ πk′ /k : Uk′ =
π−1
  k′ /k
        (U) → R is harmonic by [Thu05, Proposition 2.3.18]. We have πk′ /k (∂ Uk′ ) = ∂ U by
Lemma 2.12, so u ◦ πk′ /k ≤ h ◦ πk′ /k on ∂ Uk′ . Since u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic in Thuillier’s
sense, this implies u ◦ πk′ /k ≤ h ◦ πk′ /k on Uk′ , so u ≤ h on U since πk′ /k : Uk′ ↠ U is
surjective. Thus u is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense, as claimed.                          □
```

## GR3.14--3.15

Source arXiv:2506.13548v2,17September2025, W.Gubler/J.Rabinoff. Text SHA 3a221b86116604c950db07e4c60ab184701f0624deebabd92502e5c81871436b. LF 1062--1103. Complete3.14 statement and3.15 statement/proof plus remark.3.14 derives both-sign properties from3.5; scalar case proof is supplied above.

```text
Proposition 3.14. Let X be a k-analytic curve and let h: X → R be a continuous function.
     (1) (Sheafiness) The harmonic functions form a sheaf on X .
     (2) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX× ) then log | f | is harmonic.
     (3) The harmonic functions on X form a real vector space.
     (4) (Limits) If h is locally a uniform limit of harmonic functions, then it is harmonic.
     (5) (Maximum Principle) If h is harmonic and h attains a local extremum at x ∈ Int(X ),
         then h is constant in a neighborhood of x.
     (6) (Extension of Scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. Then h is harmonic
         if and only if h ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R is harmonic.
     (7) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic curve and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If h is
         harmonic then h ◦ f is harmonic, and the converse holds if f is finite and surjective.
     (8) (Boundaries) If h is harmonic on Int(X ), then it is harmonic on X .
   We will later see in Theorem 7.6 that (4) holds even for pointwise limits. The fol-
lowing proposition asserts that Definition 3.13 is equivalent to the notion of harmonicity
in [GJR21, Definition 7.2]. We assume that k is non-trivially valued as in ibid.
Proposition 3.15. Let X be a strictly k-analytic curve and let h: X → R be a continuous
function. The function h is harmonic if and only if it is R-PL and the line bundle Lh (x) ∈
Pic(red(X , x))R defined in (2.17) is numerically trivial for every point x ∈ X .
Proof. This is [GJR21, Proposition 15.7] in the rig-smooth case; we reduce to this case

   Suppose that h is harmonic. Then h ◦ f is harmonic by Proposition 3.14(6,7), so h ◦ f
is R-PL and Lh (x ′ ) is numerically trivial for all x ′ ∈ Xe ′ by the rig-smooth case. Then
```

## GR4.1--4.2

Source arXiv:2506.13548v2,17September2025, W.Gubler/J.Rabinoff. Text SHA 3a221b86116604c950db07e4c60ab184701f0624deebabd92502e5c81871436b. LF 1105--1177. Definition/classical curve equivalence and complete4.2 statement/proof. Only scalar descent(5) is proposed here; finite-map(6) is not a new premise of the integration.

```text
                     4. CLASSICALLY PLURISUBHARMONIC FUNCTIONS
  In this section we define the notion of a classically plurisubharmonic function. The
definition follows Definition 3.4.
Definition 4.1 (Classically Plurisubharmonic Functions). Let X be a k-analytic space and
let u: X → R ∪ {−∞} be an upper semicontinuous function. We say that u is classically
plurisubharmonic or classically psh provided that, for every analytic extension field k′ /k,
every k′ -analytic curve Y , and every morphism f : Y → X k′ , the function u◦πk′ /k ◦ f : Y →
R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic on Y in the sense of Definition 3.4.
4.1.1. Any class of psh functions should respect extension of scalars and should be stable
under pullback, so the conditions in Definition 4.1 are necessary. In complex analysis,
this condition (without the extension of scalars) is the definition of plurisubharmonicity,
which is why we call the notion “classically psh”.
4.1.2. If X is a k-analytic curve, then a function u: X → R∪{−∞} is classically psh if and
only if it is subharmonic. Indeed, if u is classically psh then taking k′ = k and Y = X in
Definition 4.1 shows that u is subharmonic, and if u is subharmonic then it is classically
psh by Proposition 3.5(7,8).
4.1.3. In Definition 4.1, one can assume that Y is a connected, smooth, strictly k′ -analytic
curve: this follows from Definition 3.4.
  Classically psh functions satisfy the basic properties of subharmonic functions on curves.
Proposition 4.2. Let X be a k-analytic space.
   (1) (Sheafiness) The classically psh functions form a sheaf on X .
   (2) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX ) then log | f |: X → R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh.
   (3) (Cone Property) If u1 , u2 : X → R∪{−∞} are classically psh and λ1 , λ2 ∈ R≥0 then
       λ1 u1 + λ2 u2 and max{u1 , u2 } are classically psh.
   (4) (Limits) The infimum of a decreasing net of classically psh functions is classically
       psh. For arbitrary pointwise limits, we refer to Theorem 6.5.
   (5) (Extension of Scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. A function u: X →
       R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh if and only if u ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R ∪ {−∞} is classically
       psh.
   (6) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic space and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If
       u: X → R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh then u ◦ f is classically psh, and the converse
       holds if f is finite and surjective.
Proof. Properties (1,3,4) follow immediately from Proposition 3.5(1,4,5). For Proposi-
tion 4.2(2), we consider an analytic function f on X . As | f | is continuous, we deduce

22                                  W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

that log | f | is a continuous function with values in R ∪ {−∞} and hence it is upper semi-
continuous. Then Proposition 4.2(2) follows from Proposition 3.5(2).
    To prove Proposition 4.2(5), it is clear from the definitions that if u is classically psh,
then u ◦ πk′ /k is classically psh for every analytic extension field k′ /k. To prove the con-
verse, we assume that k′ /k is an analytic extension field and that u′ := u ◦ πk′ /k is a
classically psh function on X k′ . Since the morphism πk′ /k is closed and surjective 2.9,
it is clear that u is upper semicontinuous. Let k′′ /k be an analytic extension field and
let f : Y → X k′′ be a morphism over k′′ from a k′′ -analytic curve Y . We have to show
that u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic. As in the proof of Proposition 3.5(7), we consider
a simultaneous analytic extension field K of k′ and k′′ . It follows from the above that
u ◦ πK/k = u′ ◦ πK/k′ is classically psh and hence u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K is subharmonic. We con-
clude from the commutative diagram (3.10.1) that u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K = u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ◦ πK/k′′ is
subharmonic and hence u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic by Proposition 3.5(7).
    Likewise, the proof of Proposition 4.2(6) follows the same lines as the proof of Propo-
sition 3.5(8). Again, it follows from the definitions if u is a classically psh function on X ,
then u ◦ f is a classically psh function on Y . Conversely, we assume that u ◦ f is a clas-
sically psh function for a finite surjective morphism f : Y → X . Since finite morphisms
are proper and hence closed, we deduce from upper semicontinuity of u ◦ f and from
surjectivity of f that u is upper semicontinuous. It remains to show that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g is
subharmonic for any analytic extension field k′ /k and any morphism g : Z → X k′ from a
connected k′ -analytic curve Z. Setting W := Z ×X k′ Yk′ , we get a morphism g ′ : W → Yk′
from f by base change with respect to f ′ : Yk′ → X k′ as in (3.10.2). Since u ◦ f is classi-
cally psh, it follows from Proposition 4.2(5) that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ′ = u ◦ f ◦ πk′ /k is classically
psh. The morphism f is finite and surjective, hence the same holds for f ′ . We con-
clude that the base change f ′′ : W → Z of the morphism f ′ is finite and surjective. The
k′ -analytic space W has irreducible components of dimension 1 and maybe isolated irre-
ducible components of dimension 0. Removing the latter, we obtain a k′ -analytic curve
W ′ as an open and closed subset of W . Since Z is a connected k′ -analytic curve and f ′′
is a finite surjective map from W onto Z, we get an induced finite surjective morphism
W ′ → Z. Indeed, as W ′ is also Zariski-closed in W , we deduce from finiteness of f ′′ that
f ′′ (W ′ ) is a Zariski-closed subset in Z of dimension 1 and hence f ′′ (W ′ ) = Z as Z is a
connected k′ -analytic curve. Since the inclusion W ′ → W is obviously finite, the com-
position W ′ → Z is also finite. In the following, we replace W by W ′ , which preserves
the commutativity of the diagram (3.10.2) and has the advantage that W is a k′ -analytic
curve. It follows that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ′ ◦ g ′ = u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g ◦ f ′′ is subharmonic on W . The
morphism of k′ -analytic curves f ′′ : W → Z is finite and surjective, so we deduce from
Proposition 3.5(8) that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g is subharmonic.                                           □
```

END MATHEMATICAL DATA

    "premises": [
      "For a k-affinoid algebra A, A is noetherian, every ideal is closed, M(A) → Spec(A) is surjective, and its spectral seminorm vanishes exactly on nilpotent elements. If A is reduced, its spectral seminorm is a norm equivalent to its Banach norm. Strict affinoid algebras admit bounded quotient presentations by unit-radius Tate algebras. [CLD 11.1.1–2]",
      "If K is complete and nontrivially valued and A is a strict K-affinoid algebra, the residue field A/m at every maximal ideal m is finite over K. More generally, A/q is finite-dimensional over K when rad(q) is maximal. [BGR 6.1.2/1; Bosch, Lectures on Formal and Rigid Geometry, 1.4, Proposition 3]",
      "For an affinoid space M(A), a rational system |f_i| ≤ r_i|g| with positive real r_i and no common zero of g,f_1,…,f_s defines an affinoid domain with its rational localization algebra. Every point has a basis of such neighbourhoods, and inside any prescribed ordinary neighbourhood the system can be chosen with all corresponding inequalities strict at that point. [CLD 11.1.5]",
      "Every ordinary open in a Berkovich analytic space is an analytic domain with its canonical restricted structure. A good analytic space has an affinoid-neighbourhood basis. Over a complete nontrivially valued field, every point of a good strict analytic space has a strict affinoid neighbourhood. Analytic-domain structures satisfy their stated factorization universal property. [CLD 11.1.5–8, 11.7.7]",
      "Analytic dimension is invariant under complete scalar extension and reduction. For a strict affinoid it equals the Krull dimension of its algebra. Local dimension is the maximum dimension of the irreducible components containing the point, and analytic-domain restriction preserves local dimension. [CLD 11.2.3]",
      "Affinoid analytic spaces have finitely many irreducible components. An irreducible analytic space is pure-dimensional, and its proper Zariski-closed subsets have smaller dimension. Closed analytic subspaces of M(A) are M(A/I), and reduction has the same carrier. [CLD 11.2.2–5]",
      "For a k-affinoid algebra A, forgetting the norm gives an equivalence between finite Banach A-algebras and finite ring A-algebras; the finite-module quotient topology is independent, up to equivalent norms, of the chosen finite presentation. Such algebras are k-affinoid. If k is nontrivially valued and A is strict, an existing Banach A-algebra finite as a ring A-algebra has this finite-module topology. [CLD 11.4.4]",
      "Finite analytic morphisms are boundaryless, are stable under composition and analytic base change, and have affinoid inverse images of affinoid domains with finite Banach algebra maps. A morphism is finite at a point precisely when it is finite from an analytic neighbourhood of that point to an analytic neighbourhood of its image. [CLD 11.4.4]",
      "Normalization of an analytic space is finite and surjective; on affinoids it is obtained from the integral closure of the reduced algebra in its total quotient ring, and normalization is local under domain restriction. [CLD 11.3.1 and the normalization construction]",
      "For an analytic morphism f:Y → X, dim_y(f) is the local dimension of its actual H(f(y))-analytic fibre and is Zariski upper semicontinuous. The morphism is finite at y if and only if dim_y(f)=0 and y belongs to Int(Y/X). [CLD 11.4.5]",
      "For an analytic-domain inclusion, relative interior is ordinary topological interior. For Z → Y → X, Int(Z/Y) ∩ g^(-1)Int(Y/X) is contained in Int(Z/X), with equality when Y → X is locally separated. Relative interiors satisfy the scalar-base-change inclusion, and boundaryless morphisms are stable under composition and analytic base change. Algebraic analytifications over their ground affinoid base are boundaryless. [CLD 11.4.1; Conrad–Temkin, Lemma 2.9; Berkovich 1.5.5]",
      "Regularity and the stated Serre conditions on coherent modules are preserved and detected by analytic-domain restriction. Quasi-smoothness is geometric regularity and is preserved by complete scalar extension. On good spaces, smoothness is quasi-smoothness together with relative interior. [CLD 11.3.1, 11.4.8–9]",
      "A regular element of an affinoid algebra remains regular on every affinoid domain; its nonempty zero space on a pure r-dimensional analytic space is pure of dimension r−1. For a morphism from a pure n-dimensional analytic space to A^d, every fibre point has local fibre dimension at least n−d, with equality when the target point has d_k-invariant d. [CLD 13.6.1, 13.7.1, 11.5.2]",
      "Ordinary CLD smooth forms admit ordinary-local analytic-unit moment presentations with ambient smooth superform coefficients. Closed paralinéaire coefficient spaces have the stated ambient extension property. Compact-image nullity characterizes zero sections. Pullback, exterior products, J, d′ and d″ have the stated sheaf, functoriality, Leibniz and differential identities. [CLD 1.9.4, 3.3.3, 3.3.10–12, 3.4.4, 6.2.1–2, 15.1.1]",
      "On a locally holomorphically separated analytic space, every compact set K inside an ordinary open U has an ordinary smooth cutoff equal to one near K and with compact support contained in U. Good Hausdorff analytic spaces are locally holomorphically separated. [CLD 15.2.2, 15.2.5]",
      "Positive ordinary CLD currents have local Radon-measure coefficients and order zero; multiplication by locally bounded Borel scalar functions is defined. Wedging a positive current with a strongly positive smooth form preserves positivity. [CLD 7.2.7–12]",
      "On a pure calibrated analytic tropical space, Stokes holds for compact C1 forms of type (n−1,n). Green’s formula holds for symmetric C2 forms of types (p,p),(q,q) with p+q=n−1 and compact intersection of supports. On boundaryless spaces, ordinary integration currents commute with differentiation, the fundamental integration current is closed, and current operations satisfy dual differential, Leibniz, weak-continuity and domain-restriction rules. [CLD 5.11.1, 7.1, 7.7, 15.7.1–2]",
      "Smooth CLD top forms determine locally finite signed Radon measures, with positive measures for positive forms, and compact smooth top forms are integrable. Canonical integration on a pure analytic space is integration of its associated cycle with actual generic Artinian lengths; these lengths and canonical calibrations are compatible with analytic-domain restriction. The intrinsic analytic and domain tropical structures coincide. [CLD 5.8.1–5, 13.2.6, 14.3.5, 15.7.1–2, 16.6.3]",
      "The support of an ordinary smooth CLD form of type (p,q) is contained in the locus d_k(x) ≥ max(p,q). Consequently it avoids any closed analytic subset of dimension less than max(p,q). Superforms on a paralinéaire space of dimension less than max(p,q) vanish in that bidegree. [CLD 1.9.9, 15.2.1]",
      "If X is compact and Γ-strict of dimension d and f_1,…,f_s are analytic units, their simultaneous norm image is a c-polytope of dimension at most d; the norm image of the analytic boundary is contained in a c-polytope of dimension at most d−1, where c=(Q,(|k×|·Γ)^Q). [Ducros, Espaces de Berkovich, polytopes, squelettes et théorie des modèles, Theorem 3.2]",
      "For a compact analytic X, an analytic morphism f:X → Y, an analytic-unit moment g, and a rigid y in Y, there are a neighbourhood U of y and a polyhedron Δ contained in g_trop(X_y), of dimension at most dim(X_y)−1, such that g_trop(X_u) is contained in g_trop(X_y) and g_trop(X_u∩∂X) is contained in Δ for all u in U. [CLD 14.7.1]",
      "Let X be compact, pure n-dimensional and S1, let Y be regular and pure one-dimensional, and let f:X → Y be pure of relative dimension n−1. For an analytic-unit moment g and a rigid y in Y, the calibrated (n−1)-dimensional fibre moment image is constant near y. If Y=A^1 and y=0, the calibrated image of the moment (g,f) on f≠0, sufficiently far in the negative log|f| direction, is the product of the calibrated fibre image and the standard interval calibration. [CLD 14.7.2, specialized to d=1]",
      "For a level-n analytic space and an n-dimensional convenient moment cell, a torus projection injective on its tropical image is finite flat of a constant positive degree on the cell interior. Canonical cell calibrations use those actual fibre degrees. Under complete scalar extension, the degrees sum over the scalar-extended fibre points and the pushforward of the canonical skeletal calibration equals the original calibration. [CLD 13.3.1–3, 14.2.2, 14.3.2–6]",
      "For a complete scalar extension L/k and scalar projection p:X_L → X, canonical top-form measures satisfy p_*((p^*ω)_Bor)=ω_Bor, and, for locally integrable ω, p_*((p^*ω)_Rad)=ω_Rad. This is scalar extension with each space’s canonical calibration and has no factor [L:k]. [CLD 15.3.1]",
      "If F/k is a finite valued extension and X is an F-analytic space of level n, viewing X as k-analytic on the same carrier preserves dimension and skeletal subsets, and its k-canonical calibration is [F:k] times its F-canonical calibration. [CLD 14.4.3]",
      "Over an algebraically closed complete non-Archimedean field, every one-dimensional normal connected strict affinoid space is an affinoid subspace of a nonsingular complete algebraic curve over the same field. [van der Put, The class group of a one-dimensional affinoid space, Theorem 1.1 and field conventions]",
      "Let K be algebraically closed, complete and nontrivially valued, let C be a smooth proper algebraic K-curve, and let W be an ordinary open of C^an. A finite continuous real function on W whose CLD current d′d″[u] is positive is Thuillier-subharmonic. The scalar tests in this current convention have compact support and algebraic very-affine unit moment presentations. [Wanner, arXiv:1801.04713v3, Definitions 3.11 and 3.16, Theorem 4.7]",
      "In GR, an analytic curve is a separated pure one-dimensional analytic space. An upper semicontinuous u is subharmonic precisely when, for every nontrivially valued analytic extension k′/k and every morphism from a smooth connected strict k′-analytic curve to X_k′, its composite is Thuillier-subharmonic or identically −∞. A real-valued h is harmonic precisely when both h and −h are subharmonic, and harmonic functions form a sheaf. [GR 3.1, Definitions 3.4 and 3.13, Proposition 3.14(1)]",
      "On a smooth connected strict analytic curve over a nontrivially valued complete field, finite GR-subharmonicity is equivalent to Thuillier-subharmonicity. On a smooth strict curve, a real function is Thuillier-harmonic if and only if both signs are subharmonic. [GR 3.4.2 and 3.12]",
      "On a smooth strict curve over a nontrivially valued complete field, Thuillier subharmonicity is the upper-semicontinuous, componentwise non-identically-minus-infinity comparison property against harmonic functions on every strict affinoid domain. Harmonic pullback is preserved under every complete non-Archimedean field extension, with the affinoid convention H(U)=Γ(U∖∂U,H_X)∩C0(U,R). For a quasi-compact simply semistable formal curve, membership of a continuous function in its harmonic space is equivalent to membership of its scalar pullback in the base-changed harmonic space. [Thuillier Definition 3.1.5, Proposition 2.3.18 and Lemma 2.3.5]",
      "For a reduced strict analytic curve, its finite normalization is rig-smooth when the field is perfect, is an isomorphism outside a discrete set of rigid points, and detects R-PL functions: h is R-PL if and only if h composed with normalization is R-PL. [GR Lemma 3.9(1,2,6)]",
      "For any complete non-Archimedean field k, any k-analytic curve X, any continuous h:X → R and any analytic field extension k′/k, h is GR-harmonic if and only if h composed with X_k′ → X is GR-harmonic. No smoothness, strictness, reducedness, perfectness, separability or algebraicity hypothesis is imposed. [GR Proposition 3.14(6)]",
      "For a morphism f:Y → X of analytic curves over a complete non-Archimedean field, a GR-harmonic function h on X pulls back to a GR-harmonic function h∘f on Y; the converse holds when f is finite and surjective. No smoothness, reducedness or separability hypothesis is imposed. [GR Proposition 3.14(7)]",
      "On any k-analytic space, classical psh means upper semicontinuity and subharmonicity of every composite obtained from every analytic field extension k′/k, every k′-analytic curve Y and every morphism Y → X_k′. On an analytic curve, classical psh is equivalent to GR-subharmonicity. A real-valued function is classically pluriharmonic precisely when both signs are classically psh. [GR Definition 4.1, 4.1.2 and Definition 7.1]",
      "For any k-analytic space X and any analytic field extension k′/k, a function u:X → R∪{−∞} is classically psh if and only if its scalar pullback to X_k′ is classically psh. [GR Proposition 4.2(5)]",
      "For any analytic space X and analytic field extension k′/k, scalar projection is closed and surjective, and π_k′/k(∂X_k′)=∂X. An analytic extension field is a complete non-Archimedean field equipped with an isometric embedding of the ground field. [GR 2.1–2.2, 2.9 and Lemma 2.12]",
      "Let k be complete and nontrivially valued, X a strict k-analytic curve and h:X → R continuous. Then h is GR-harmonic if and only if h is R-PL and its residual class L_h(x) in Pic(red(X,x))_R is numerically trivial for every x. R-PL has the G-local finite-real-linear-combination-of-logarithms-of-analytic-units convention on strict analytic domains. The statement permits singular and nonreduced curves and imperfect fields. [GR 2.16–2.17 and Proposition 3.15]",
      "For a good strict analytic space over a complete nontrivially valued field, GJR R-PL means representation by finite real linear combinations of logarithms of analytic units on a G-cover generated by strict affinoid domains. An R-PL function is GJR-harmonic precisely when both signs are semipositive at every point, equivalently when every residual line-bundle class is numerically trivial. [GJR 2.2, Definition 5.1, Definition 7.2 and Remark 7.3(1)]",
      "For a good strict analytic space over a complete nontrivially valued field, Λ-PL is insensitive to reduction. If Λ is divisible and K′ is the completion of an algebraic valued extension of K, a function is Λ-PL if and only if its scalar pullback to X_K′ is Λ-PL. [GJR v3 Lemma 5.4(3) and Corollary 5.12]",
      "For a Λ-PL function on a good strict analytic space over a complete nontrivially valued field, semipositivity and harmonicity are preserved by analytic morphisms; the global converse holds for proper surjective morphisms. They are also preserved and detected globally by every analytic field extension and by reduction. These detection statements presuppose the stated Λ-PL hypotheses. [GJR Proposition 7.6(1′,2′,3′)]",

---
title: "Bounded generated potentials and the unresolved Bedford--Taylor equality"
author: "P21.3 continuation 95 research draft"
date: "5 October 2026"
geometry: margin=1in
fontsize: 11pt
header-includes:
  - \setlength{\emergencystretch}{3em}

# Status and exact scope

**Original P21.3 remains unresolved in this draft.** We prove the proposed bounded-generated-Psh multiplier lemma, including its Borel multiplication and ordinary sheafification steps, without assuming continuous-Psh recognition. We also prove a consequence of primary classical pluriharmonic geometry: on a fixed compact affinoid, every linear subspace of classical pluriharmonic functions is closed under pointwise convergent nets, and such convergence is uniform. The second result diagnoses an approximation route; it gives no new Bedford--Taylor coverage. Neither result proves preservation of an arbitrary signed curvature relation, and no admissible analytic counterexample is constructed. These are unverified research proofs, with no priority claim.

Write $D=\mathrm d'\mathrm d''$. The unchanged target allows every complete non-Archimedean field $K$, including a trivially valued field. The space $X$ is good, separated, strict, boundaryless and pure of analytic dimension $n\geq2$. On **every ordinary open** $W\subset X$, the current $T$ has ordinary CLD bidegree $(p,p)$, $0\leq p\leq n-1$, is middle-positive, and satisfies both $\mathrm d'T=0$ and $\mathrm d''T=0$. For all finite continuous sections $U,V$ of the generated CLD Psh sheaf, the requested implication is

$$D[U]=D[V]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad D((U-V)T|_W)=0. \tag{1}$$

Here $[f]=f\delta_X$ uses the canonical integration current. In particular, a generic component is weighted by its actual generic length. We do not replace $X$ by its reduction, pull back $T$, or identify the analytic fibre product with a topological product. Positivity means symmetry and nonnegative evaluation on **middle-positive** complementary smooth tests, including the interior degrees where positivity classes differ.

# Exact external premises

The following is the complete list of non-elementary external premises used. The indices agree with premises.json. Primary references mean [CLD], arXiv:1204.6277v2 (25 July 2025); [GJR], arXiv:2111.05741v3 (7 March 2025); and [GR], arXiv:2506.13548v2 (17 September 2025). The full texts were **not** read. All selected literal statements and printed proofs, where present, are preserved in P21.3-primary-evidence.md; provenance distinguishes frozen supplied primary text from public-browser text. A cited published theorem is an external premise, not an independently verified foundational theorem.

**Premise 0: analytic and ordinary calculus scope.** Analytic spaces have compact-neighbourhood bases; every ordinary open is an analytic domain with its canonical structure [CLD 11.1.7]. A topologically separated pure $n$-dimensional analytic space has the calibrated level-$n$ tropical structure, and analytic-domain calculus agrees with restriction [CLD 15.7.1--2]. A good Hausdorff analytic space is locally holomorphically and differentially separated; compact supports admit smooth cutoffs, compact smooth localization, and uniform approximation of compact continuous scalars by smooth scalars [CLD 15.2.2--9]. This includes either valuation regime. Goodness and separatedness of the target and its ordinary opens give precisely this scope, without a global paracompactness assumption.

**Premise 1: generated Psh.** Psh is the smallest ordinary subsheaf of functions valued in $\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}$ containing smooth functions with positive smooth Hessian and closed under decreasing sequential limits [CLD Definition 8.1.1]. Its sheafified ordinal stages are: smooth generators at rank zero; ordinary-locally decreasing sequential limits of the previous stage at a successor; ordinary-local earlier-stage membership at a limit [CLD Lemma 8.1.4]. The assertion that this construction exhausts Psh, upper semicontinuity, and its cone operations are reconstructed below. The printed stabilization proof's unnecessary locally-integrable qualification is not used to change Definition 8.1.1.

**Premise 2: middle positivity and compact decomposition.** Every compact symmetric smooth $(q,q)$ test is a difference of two compact middle-positive smooth tests, when its support lies in a paracompact locally differentially separated open [CLD 6.5.2]. The theorem explicitly includes compact support on an almost-good analytic space, so good suffices here. Middle-positive currents are symmetric functionals nonnegative on middle-positive tests; positivity is ordinary-local [CLD 7.2.1--4]. A positive smooth factor wedged with a middle-positive current is middle-positive [CLD 7.2.7(b)]. No spanning assertion about strongly positive forms is used.

**Premise 3: scalar coefficient measures.** On a locally differentially separated level-$n$ space, every positive scalar current is represented by a unique positive Radon measure [CLD 7.2.8]. For a middle-positive current $T$ and a smooth middle-positive complementary form $\eta$, its scalar coefficient measure $\mu_\eta$ satisfies $\int g\,d\mu_\eta=\langle T,g\eta\rangle$ for compact smooth scalars $g$, and is compactly supported if $\eta$ is compactly supported [CLD 7.2.11]. The source states locally bounded Borel multiplication in 7.2.12. We reconstruct its middle-positive order-zero justification rather than using the broader strongly-positive inference of 7.2.9.

**Premise 4: topology and the middle cone.** In Euclidean coordinates a symmetric $(q,q)$ superform is middle-positive exactly when its signed coefficient matrix on the exterior power is positive semidefinite [CLD 1.2.6--7]. Positivity is tested on each tangent face. Test-form topology is the inductive topology of fixed compact supports and fixed moment presentations, with the cellwise coefficient seminorms; continuity may be tested after compact smooth localization to one chart [CLD 7.1.1--3]. The image of a compact analytic domain under a moment is a finite polyhedral set [CLD 12.2.4; the theorem cites its primary proofs rather than providing one]. Only this polyhedrality assertion is used, not the printed dimension-equality clause, which fails for a constant moment. This is the finite-face input for the coefficient bound below. None of these facts classifies a singular current.

**Premise 5: restriction and differential identities.** Ordinary forms have compatible restriction, exterior products and Leibniz rules [CLD 3.3.10--12]. Ordinary currents form a sheaf; compact extension by zero defines restriction [CLD 7.1.6--7]. The current differentials obey

$$ (\mathrm d')^2=(\mathrm d'')^2=0,\qquad
\mathrm d''\mathrm d'=-\mathrm d'\mathrm d'',\qquad DJ=JD,\qquad
\langle DS,\alpha\rangle=\langle S,D\alpha\rangle $$

[CLD 7.7.1--5]. For smooth $s$ and a symmetric doubly closed $T$, $D(sT)=Ds\wedge T$ [CLD 8.5.3--4]. These identities concern the actual ordinary product on $W$.

**Premise 6: canonical integration.** The boundaryless canonical integration current $\delta_X=[1]$ is positive and doubly closed [CLD 15.7.1, 7.7.4]. For a pure closed analytic subspace $Y$, $\delta_Y=\delta_{[Y]}$, with generic component multiplicities [CLD 16.6.3]. Thus applying our arguments to $\delta_X$ includes singularities and nilpotents with their original calibration.

**Premise 7: classical pluriharmonic geometry.** For any complete non-Archimedean field, including trivial valuation, [GR Definition 7.1] defines classical pluriharmonicity by requiring both signs to be classically psh. These functions are continuous and form a real vector space [GR 7.2]. On any affinoid $A$ with finite Shilov boundary $\Gamma$, a classical pluriharmonic function attains its maximum and minimum on $\Gamma$, and evaluation $\operatorname{PH}(A)\to\mathbb R^\Gamma$ is injective [GR 7.3, using the global maximum principle 6.4]. There is no good/strict/smooth/nontrivial-valued hypothesis in 7.3. We use this published analytic theorem only for functions already in $\operatorname{PH}(A)$, never to recognize a zero ordinary Hessian.

**Premise 8: diagnostics, not recognition assumptions.** [GR 1.2] gives the inclusion of generated CLD Psh in classical psh, by functoriality, decreasing limits and the smooth-curve comparison. [GR 7.7--8] requires a nontrivially valued field and an **actual** strictly semistable formal model of a good quasicompact strict space to deduce real-PL regularity of already classically pluriharmonic functions. [GJR 10.17] instead starts with weak smoothness and a zero weak **form** Hessian, in its nontrivially valued good strict scope, and concludes harmonic real-PL regularity. [GJR 12.1--2] provides restriction of strong currents to ordinary tests, not a positive closed lift of every current and not product compatibility. These are used solely to audit the attempted routes; none supplies an assumption in Theorem 1 below.

We use bounded convergence for finite positive measures in its usual exact form: Borel $f_j\to f$ pointwise, with a common finite absolute bound on a finite-measure support, implies convergence of their integrals. A short proof is included in the successor argument, so it is not an additional external premise.

# Ordinary Borel multiplication for middle-positive currents

**Lemma 1.** Let $T$ be middle-positive on an allowed ordinary open. For each compact symmetric complementary test $\eta$ there is a canonical finite signed Radon measure $\mu_\eta$, linear in $\eta$, supported in $\operatorname{supp}\eta$, with

$$ \int g\,d\mu_\eta=\langle T,g\eta\rangle $$

for smooth $g$. Every locally bounded real Borel $f$ defines a symmetric ordinary order-zero current $fT$ by integration against these measures. It agrees with smooth multiplication and commutes with ordinary restriction. If $f_j$ have one finite common bound on every compact and converge pointwise to $f$, then $f_jT\to fT$ weakly.

**Proof.** Decompose $\eta=\eta_1-\eta_2$ using Premise 2. The coefficient measures of Premise 3 give $\mu_\eta=\mu_{\eta_1}-\mu_{\eta_2}$. Two decompositions give the same integrals against compact smooth scalars, hence against compact continuous scalars by smooth density, hence the same Radon measure. This also proves linearity. The functional vanishes on scalars supported away from $\operatorname{supp}\eta$. Density within that complementary open shows that $\mu_\eta$ is zero there. Thus the apparent larger supports of a positive decomposition do not enlarge the signed measure's intrinsic support. For an arbitrary test use its symmetric part: the antisymmetric part pairs to zero with $T$. This produces a linear symmetric functional

$$\langle fT,\eta\rangle=\int f\,d\mu_\eta. \tag{2}$$

We check that this functional is a current, including when $f$ is discontinuous. Work on a fixed chart $g:O\to E$, after multiplication by a compact nonnegative smooth scalar $\rho$. Choose one compact analytic domain $C\subset O$ containing $\operatorname{supp}\rho$ in its interior. The compact moment image $P=g_{\rm trop}(C)$ has finitely many tangent faces. On each face of dimension at least $q$, let $\Theta=\sum_i\mathrm d'x_i\wedge\mathrm d''x_i$ for fixed Euclidean coordinates on $E$. The coefficient matrix of $\Theta^q$ on $\bigwedge^q$ of that tangent space is positive definite. Consequently there is a constant $c$, for this finite presentation, such that for every symmetric presented test $a$,

$$ c\|a\|_{P,0}\Theta^q+a\geq0,\qquad
   c\|a\|_{P,0}\Theta^q-a\geq0. \tag{3}$$

Indeed on each of the finitely many tangent spaces the operator norm of the signed coefficient matrix of $a$ is bounded by a fixed multiple of its coefficient norm, whereas the fixed matrix of $\Theta^q$ has a positive minimum eigenvalue. Take the maximum of these finitely many constants. On faces of dimension below $q$ both restrictions vanish. For $q=0$, use $\Theta^0=1$ and scalar inequalities. This proof concerns the middle cone only.

Pull back (3) and multiply by $\rho$. The resulting compact forms are middle-positive. Their positive scalar coefficient measures imply the measure inequalities

$$-c\|a\|_{P,0}\mu_{\rho g^*\Theta^q}
  \leq\mu_{\rho g^*a}
  \leq c\|a\|_{P,0}\mu_{\rho g^*\Theta^q}.$$

The usual positive/negative decomposition of a signed measure gives a total variation bound by the measure on the right. Equivalently, integrate scalar simple functions of either sign and then bounded Borel limits. Thus

$$ |\langle fT,\rho g^*a\rangle|
 \leq c\|f\|_{\operatorname{supp}\rho}\|a\|_{P,0}
       \langle T,\rho g^*\Theta^q\rangle. \tag{4}$$

The factor involving $f$ is finite by local boundedness and compactness. The right hand side is a fixed $C^0$ seminorm bound on this test family. The symmetric projection is continuous, so the same conclusion holds for arbitrary tests. Finite smooth localization and Premise 4 prove ordinary order-zero continuity. This replaces the inappropriate use of strongly positive spanning in the broader source proof of 7.2.9.

For an ordinary inclusion $N\subset W$, extend a compact test on $N$ by zero. Equality of its scalar coefficient measures, checked on smooth scalars and then by density, proves $(fT)|_N=f|_N(T|_N)$. Smooth $f$ agrees with the original product by construction. Finally, for any fixed compact test, its two positive coefficient measures are finite. The stated common compact bounds allow bounded convergence separately for each measure in its positive decomposition. This proves weak convergence. $\square$

# Full sheafified ordinal reconstruction

We first reconstruct the stages and measurability. Let $\mathcal P_0$ be the ordinary sheaf of smooth positive-Hessian functions. Define $\mathcal P_{\xi+1}$ by ordinary-local decreasing limits of sequences from $\mathcal P_\xi$. At a limit $\lambda$, define membership by ordinary-local membership in some $\mathcal P_\xi$, $\xi<\lambda$. These are sheaves: the definitions are local conditions on sections of the ambient function sheaf. They are increasing because constant sequences retain a preceding section, and earlier stages embed in a limit.

Every stage consists of upper-semicontinuous functions with values in $\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}$. At a successor, for $f=\inf_j f_j$,

$$\{f<a\}=\bigcup_j\{f_j<a\}$$

is open. At a limit upper semicontinuity is ordinary-local. Hence every finite section is Borel. No second countability of the analytic space is needed: the preimages of the countable rational intervals generate the real Borel sigma-algebra.

The stages exhaust the minimal Psh sheaf. The collection of all pairs (ordinary open, section of the ambient function sheaf on that open) is a set. An increasing chain cannot introduce a distinct pair at every successor beyond its cardinal successor. At a stationary stage $\mathcal P_\kappa=\mathcal P_{\kappa+1}$, the construction is fixed at all subsequent successors and limits. This stage is closed under decreasing sequences on a fixed open: their limit belongs at rank $\kappa+1=\kappa$, without any integrability restriction. It contains the generators and is a sheaf, so minimality puts Psh inside it. Conversely every stage lies in Psh, by its two defining closure conditions and ordinary locality. This proves equality. Restricting these local constructions to an ordinary open gives the same local stages there; all induction statements below are made simultaneously on every ordinary open of $X$.

**Theorem 1 (bounded-generated multiplier positivity).** Under every original ambient hypothesis, let $f$ be a locally bounded real section of generated Psh on any ordinary $W$. Its Borel ordinary product is defined, and

$$D(fT|_W)\quad\text{is middle-positive and doubly closed}. \tag{5}$$

In particular this holds for every finite continuous generated-Psh potential, without recognizing it as CPsh.

**Proof.** Transfinitely, simultaneously for every ordinary open and every middle-positive doubly closed current in the specified degree, prove the assertion for every locally bounded real section of $\mathcal P_\xi$.

At rank zero, Premise 5 gives $D(fT)=Df\wedge T$. The smooth Hessian is positive; Premise 2 gives middle positivity.

At a successor fix a locally bounded real final section $f$. Around any point take an ordinary neighbourhood $N$ on which $f_j\in\mathcal P_\xi(N)$ decrease to $f$. Restricting $T$ to $N$ retains positivity and both closedness equations. At each point, $f_j\geq f$ implies that every $f_j$ is finite: the stages allow no $+\infty$, and $-\infty$ would contradict the lower bound. The final section bounds each $f_j$ locally below. Every finite upper-semicontinuous $f_j$ bounds itself above near each point, using the open set $\{f_j<f_j(x)+1\}$. Thus each $f_j$ is locally bounded on $N$. The inductive theorem applies to each, although it can be discontinuous and can itself have only sheaf-local preceding representations.

Fix a compact smooth symmetric test $\alpha$ of bidegree $(n-p-1,n-p-1)$ on $N$. The compact symmetric complementary form $D\alpha$ has a decomposition $\eta_1-\eta_2$ into compact middle-positive forms. Let $\mu_i$ be the associated finite positive coefficient measures of the **fixed original** $T|_N$. For the compact union $C$ of their supports, local boundedness of $f$ and upper semicontinuity of the finite $f_1$ give finite constants $a,b$ such that

$$ a\leq f\leq f_j\leq f_1\leq b\quad\hbox{on }C,\qquad j\geq1. \tag{6}$$

Upper boundedness of $f_1$ on $C$ follows by its local upper bounds and a finite subcover; boundedness of $f$ follows similarly. The support and bounds are fixed before $j$.

For completeness, convergence of the two integrals follows without a current regularization. Put $g_j=f_j-f$ on $C$; then $0\leq g_j\leq b-a$ and $g_j\downarrow0$. For $\epsilon>0$, the Borel sets $E_j=\{g_j\geq\epsilon\}$ decrease to the empty set. Finiteness of $\mu_i$ gives $\mu_i(E_j)\to0$: write the disjoint successive differences and use countable additivity. Therefore

$$0\leq\int g_j\,d\mu_i
  \leq\epsilon\mu_i(C)+(b-a)\mu_i(E_j).$$

First let $j$ tend to infinity and then $\epsilon$ tend to zero. Formula (2) and differential duality now give

$$ \langle D(f_jT),\alpha\rangle
     =\int f_j\,d\mu_1-\int f_j\,d\mu_2
     \longrightarrow
       \int f\,d\mu_1-\int f\,d\mu_2
     =\langle D(fT),\alpha\rangle. \tag{7}$$

For a middle-positive $\alpha$, every left hand side is nonnegative by induction. Its limit is therefore nonnegative. The current $fT$ is symmetric and $DJ=JD$, so its Hessian is symmetric. This proves middle positivity on $N$. Lemma 1's restriction compatibility and ordinary locality of positive currents prove it on $W$.

At a limit $\lambda$, around each point $f$ belongs to some earlier $\mathcal P_\xi$ with $\xi<\lambda$. Local boundedness restricts there. The inductive assertion gives positivity there, and locality gives it on $W$. There is no choice of one earlier rank for all of $W$, and no intersection of infinitely many neighbourhoods.

Stabilization gives the assertion for Psh. For any current $S$, $\mathrm d'DS=0$ by $(\mathrm d')^2=0$, and $\mathrm d''DS=-\mathrm d'(\mathrm d'')^2S=0$. Hence (5) is doubly closed. All degree endpoints are included: for $p=n-1$ the $\alpha$ tests are scalars; for $p=0$ their degree is $(n-1,n-1)$. The proof has not modified the calibration, assumed reducedness, or used a nontrivial valuation. $\square$

**Corollary 1.** Given locally bounded real generated-Psh sections $f_1,\ldots,f_m$ and $m\leq n-p$, the ordered recursion

$$T_0=T,\qquad T_k=D(f_kT_{k-1})\quad(1\leq k\leq m)$$

defines middle-positive doubly closed ordinary currents in degree $(p+k,p+k)$.

**Proof.** Apply Theorem 1 at each step while $p+k-1\leq n-1$. Products are the Borel order-zero products of Lemma 1 for the newly positive current. $\square$

This is an ordered construction. We prove neither independence of the order nor independence from curvature-equivalent potentials, so it must not be advertised as a canonical mixed Hessian product.

**Corollary 2.** If $h$ is locally bounded and both $h$ and $-h$ are generated Psh, then $D(hT)=0$ for every target $T$.

**Proof.** Theorem 1 makes $R=D(hT)$ and $-R=D((-h)T)$ middle-positive. Every middle-positive test pairs to zero with $R$. Symmetric-test decomposition handles arbitrary symmetric tests, and symmetry handles antisymmetric tests. Thus $R=0$. $\square$

For the actual $h=U-V$, only $U,V$ separately belong to Psh. Equation $D[h]=0$ has not supplied the two memberships in Corollary 2. In particular (5) gives two positive currents $D(UT)$ and $D(VT)$, not their equality.

# A consequence of actual affinoid pluriharmonic geometry

**Theorem 2 (closed subspaces under nets).** Let $A$ be any compact $K$-affinoid space over any complete non-Archimedean field. Let $L$ be any real linear subspace of the space $\operatorname{PH}(A)$ of classical pluriharmonic functions. If a net $(h_a)$ in $L$ converges pointwise on $A$ to a finite real function $h$, then $h\in L$ and the convergence is uniform on $A$. The limit need not be assumed continuous.

**Proof.** Let $\Gamma$ be the finite Shilov boundary. Premise 7 gives the linear injective evaluation map $E:\operatorname{PH}(A)\to\mathbb R^\Gamma$. For $v\in\operatorname{PH}(A)$, the maximum and minimum statements give

$$\|v\|_{C(A)}=\max_{x\in\Gamma}|v(x)|. \tag{8}$$

The image $E(L)$ is a linear subspace of the finite-dimensional Euclidean space $\mathbb R^\Gamma$, hence closed. To see this elementary fact without a topological separation theorem, take a basis of $E(L)$, extend it to a basis of $\mathbb R^\Gamma$, and observe that $E(L)$ is the common zero set of the remaining coordinate functionals. Pointwise convergence on the finite $\Gamma$ is convergence in $\mathbb R^\Gamma$, also for a directed net: for a fixed tolerance, take a common upper index for the finitely many evaluation thresholds. Its limit $(h(x))_{x\in\Gamma}$ therefore equals $E(v)$ for some $v\in L$. Equation (8) applied to $h_a-v$ gives uniform convergence to $v$. Pointwise uniqueness gives $h=v$. This proves the theorem. $\square$

On a compact space covered by finitely many affinoid domains, the same argument, using the union of their Shilov boundaries, proves the same assertion for any linear subspace of global classical pluriharmonic functions. Each function restricts classically pluriharmonically to these domains, so the supremum norm is determined by this finite union. This also reconstructs the net version of the sequence mechanism in [GR 7.4, 7.6].

A precise consequence for approximation is as follows. On such a fixed $A$, let

$$L_A=\{v\in\operatorname{PH}(A):v\text{ is ordinary CLD-smooth and }Dv=0\text{ as a smooth form}\}.$$

This is a linear subspace of $\operatorname{PH}(A)$. A pointwise or uniform net limit of its elements is **already** in $L_A$ by Theorem 2. In the usual good strict CLD scope, smooth functions with zero smooth Hessian have both signs among the smooth Psh generators, and the source inclusion [GR 1.2] places them in $\operatorname{PH}(A)$. Thus passing from sequences to nets of such harmonic functions on a fixed compact affinoid cannot create nonsmooth limits. Ordinary smoothness near a point and on a compact affinoid are distinct scopes; this conclusion requires the approximants on that same affinoid, and makes no statement about index-dependent shrinking domains.

This is a **diagnostic analytic consequence**, not a counterexample to (1). The actual pair difference has not been shown classically pluriharmonic. Even an already classically pluriharmonic nonsmooth function need not violate (1): the task-granted strict real-PL theorem handles many such differences over nontrivially valued fields. No residual Picard example is claimed as a new negative result.

# Attempts at the missing equality

The induction just proved cannot be recycled for a signed pair relation by replacing $f$ with $U-V$. The smooth base has exact relation preservation, but at a successor $U_j\downarrow U$ and $V_j\downarrow V$ are separate representations. Nothing makes $D[U_j]=D[V_j]$. Although (7) permits passing the products to their respective limits, it supplies no zero product difference to pass. At a limit the corresponding earlier ranks may also differ; taking their maximum puts the two sections in a common stage but does not create a curvature relation at the predecessor stage. Sheafification does not solve this defect.

One might try to perturb $U$ by subtracting $V$, thereby creating the two signs of $h$. The generated definition gives a cone, not a vector space. For completeness its finite nonnegative combinations do persist at every ordinal: at rank zero this follows by linearity of the smooth Hessian; at a successor intersect the two representation neighbourhoods and add the two decreasing sequences; at a limit move the two sections to the maximum of their two earlier ranks on the intersected neighbourhood. Upper semicontinuity and constants persist as above. Coefficient zero is handled by omitting that summand rather than by multiplying $-\infty$ by zero. This validates positive combinations but provides no subtraction operation. Equality of **canonical** current Hessians cannot be silently substituted for a generating construction of $h$ or $-h$.

The GR route has two distinct missing implications. The input $U,V$ is classically psh by the source inclusion, but their difference is not covered by that cone inclusion. To apply the Shilov and semistable statements to $h$, one needs both signs to pass **every** classical curve test, including after every complete analytic extension. The upstairs equation $D[U]=D[V]$ is an equation of ordinary ambient currents. It does not define a pullback of that current equation to a lower-dimensional curve, or ascent of it to a transcendental extension. No such operation has been constructed here. The finite-dimensional argument of Theorem 2 starts only after the classical membership is known.

Even that membership alone does not remove all scope restrictions. [GR 7.8] further assumes a good quasicompact strict domain with an actual strictly semistable formal model over a nontrivially valued field. Its simplex interpolation proof applies to those models; it neither asserts their existence on arbitrary analytic germs nor makes arbitrary singularities smooth. The task-granted strict real-PL multiplier theorem could be applied **after** these hypotheses and recognition were verified. We have not obtained them for arbitrary $W$ and do not claim a new special case through this chain.

For current regularization, smoothing on one coarse tropical chart would have to preserve positive middle cones and both closedness equations after every additional analytic-unit moment refinement. A chartwise convolution does not come with such compatibility, a globally defined current, or local weak convergence on all ordinary analytic tests. We have constructed none of these. Similarly, a strong-current restriction map gives no lift with the required properties; even a lift would need a proof that the restricted strong product agrees with the Borel ordinary product (2). Neither theorem in this draft assumes that bridge.

There is a further obstruction to using the positivity theorem to prove recursive symmetry without another analytic argument. In an induction, bounded predecessors can be discontinuous. Weak convergence of their positive differentiated currents only controls integrals against continuous scalars; it does not justify multiplying moving currents by an arbitrary discontinuous predecessor. Theorem 1 avoids this issue because its coefficient measures belong to one **fixed** $T$. It proves the ordered recursion, not a general interchange of its factors. A capacity or compatible regularization proof would be needed before making that additional assertion.

No actual analytic negative construction was completed. In particular the known weighted cross has unequal upstairs curvatures and cannot certify the target premise, while nonsmooth real-PL harmonic functions in the granted scope already obey the ordinary multiplier equality. Theorem 2 shows why adding more indices to ordinary harmonic approximants on a fixed affinoid does not evade their smoothness restriction; it does not manufacture admissible potentials or a defective current.

# Concrete unresolved analytic statement and comparison

The genuinely established step is Theorem 1's full bounded-generated-Psh positivity, with every sheafified ordinal and all original ambient fields and current degrees. It removes the need to assume reverse CPsh recognition for **separate-product positivity**. Theorem 2 is a further rigorous diagnostic of the fixed-domain harmonic approximation route, based on an actual published analytic theorem. Neither is a new all-current equality result in a previously residual range.

One concrete sufficient analytic statement still lacking a proof is:

> For every original $K,X$, every ordinary $W$, and every finite continuous generated-Psh pair $U,V$ with $D[U]=D[V]$, each point has a fixed ordinary neighbourhood $N\subset W$ on which **both** $U-V$ and $V-U$ belong to the original generated Psh sheaf, by a valid ordinary-local sheafified generating construction.

Theorem 1 and pointedness would prove (1) from this statement, with no current approximation. This is a sufficient route, not a claimed equivalence or a new proved recognition theorem. A different proof preserving all signed curvature relations could settle P21.3 without this stronger two-sign membership statement. The missing current-regularization alternative would require local weak approximation of every allowed $T$ by actual middle-positive doubly closed ordinary currents for which relation preservation has already been proved, with compatibility on all analytic refinements. No such density theorem is supplied or proved.

The catalogue's normal-curve, componentwise, fixed-polycylinder, ordinary-basis, specified-tower, invariant split-toric, strict real-PL and explicitly weak-approximable cases are not counted as resolutions. In particular a cover of one specified open does not produce an ordinary-open basis for all opens. This draft does not identify arbitrary ambient zero Hessians with curvewise harmonicity or with weak form Hessians, and retains the trivial-valuation, singular and nonreduced parts of the original residual gap. Its status is partial and `candidate=false`.

The standalone typeset core is P21.3-paper.tex and its PDF. premises.json gives all nonnegative premise indices, exact standalone statements, applicability checks and versioned primary URLs. source-manifest.json records the source/unit hashes and inclusive one-based LF coordinates using UTF8 decoding followed by `split('\n')`. For GR, the downloaded browser text is distinguished from unavailable original PDF bytes; no original GR PDF checksum is claimed. Partial reading is recorded explicitly.

Run `bash reproduce.sh` in this directory to verify source copies, rebuild evidence and the paper, compile twice, and check the input/status/note schemas. Logs and tool versions are retained in `logs/` and environment.json. These finite checks verify artifact integrity and compilation, not infinite-rank mathematical induction or the truth of P21.3. No independent proof review or other worker/model was invoked. No resource-heavy computation was needed; no supervised heavy-compute facility was supplied or launched.
